[trialAccount t18470816-1933]
1933. THOMAS DUGDALE was indicted for unlawfully selling and publishing obscene prints.
MR. E. LAW conducted the Prosecution.
GEORGE WILLIAM SHARP. I am clerk to Messrs. Pritchard and Collett, solicitors to the Society for the Suppression of Vice, No. 57, Lincoln's Innfields. On the 27th of July last, about half-past nine o'clock in the evening, in consequence of instruction I received, I went to the station-house at Bow-street, and after being there searched by a policeman, I went to No. 51, Holywell-street, Strand, followed by the policeman—I went into the shop and asked for a book, which the prisoner showed me, and which I produce—I paid a guinea for it—I then left the shop, and went, closely followed by the policeman who had searched me, to the Society's chambers, where he took from my pocket the book I had bought of the defendant.
COURT. Q. Was it a public and open shop, that anybody might go into? A. Yes, there were prints of Venus, and all those sorts of things, in the window, to denote the nature of the traffic carried on inside.
Prisoner I deny that; I plead guilty to selling the book, and throw myself on the mercy of the Court.
WILLIAM CHADWICK ( police-sergeant F 2.) On the 27th of July, about half-past nine o'clock in the evening, I searched Mr. Sharp very strictly, and am certain he had then no book in his possession—I followed him to No. 51, Holywell-street—I never lost sight of him all the way—I was not five yards behind him—he went into the shop—I waited in view of the shop till he came out again, and then followed him to the Society's chambers, where I again searched him, and found this book in him—on the 20th of Aug. I received a warrant to arrest the prisoner—I went to his shop, and found him sitting there smoking his pipe—I was in my unigram—I took him into custody—Mr. Davis, who was with me, searched the house, and seized some articles.
COLBY ATKINSON DAVIS. I am an officer of the Customs. On the 20th of Aug. I received instruction from the Commissioners of Customs, went to No. 51, Holywell-street, and searched the premises—I there found in a box, in the front bed-room, 377 pictures of the most obscene description—I produce a part of them, the whole are in the Court, besides snuff-boxes and other things—it is the prisoner's residence—I have visited his shop several times before, and have seized books and other things before—I seize these things, on the part of the Customs, as French articles, not having paid the duty—I have no doubt of these being French goods—the snuff-boxes have false bottoms with screws—they were found in a box, of which the prisoner produced me the key from a drawer in the shop—the stock consisted of nothing but these kind of books to excite the passions, except one row of novels of about fifty volumes—these obscene works were lying alongside of Bibles and Prayer-books—the place was very poorly furnished up stairs—these pictures are brought over by the stewards of steam-boats—I once seized upwards of 30, 000 prints from the prisoner's brother, and from William Dugdale 12, 000 or 14, 000.
Prisoner's Defence. I am very poor, and am getting almost blind, and do not know what to turn to to get a living; I have got out of my own trade, which I am not able to follow; I was then engaged at my brother's till he discharged me; I have no desire to demoralize society; I whish to get my living comfortably and honourably.
GUILTY. Aged 26.— Confined One Year, and to enter into his own recognizance in 80 l. for five years.
[trialAccount t18470816-1934]
1934. JANE WADSWORTH was indicted for a like offence.
MR. E. LAW conducted the Prosecution.
GEORGE WILLIAM SHARP. On the 13th of July, in consequence of instruction I had received, after being searched at the station-house, I went to No. 20, Wych-street, Drury-lane—I went into the shop, saw the prisoner there, and asked for a book—I did not ask for it by name—I paid a guines for it, and left—the officer followed me, as before, to the Society's chambers, and took the book from me there—this is it.
WILLIAM CHADWICK ( police-serjeant F 2.) On the 13th of July I searched Sharp at the station-house—he had no book on him then—I followed him to
Wych-street, and saw him go into NO. 20—he came out shortly afterwards—I followed him to the Society's chambers, where I again searched him, and took from him this book.
COLBY ATKINSON DAVIS. On the 20th of Aug., in consequence of directions from the Commissioners of Customs, I went to No.20, Wych-street, and searched the house and shop—I found the prisoner there, up stairs—I seized two British books full of obscene prints, which the policeman brought away—the shop is kept by a person named Blacketer, who has just now come out of prison, where he has been three months for exposing these sort of things for sale in the window—the prisoner lives with him as his wife—this makes the fourth seizure I have made there—I never went there before without bringing away 100 or 150 of these books an pictures—we bring away the foreign, and the police take the British—as soon as I go into the shop and state what I want, he produce them I take them away, and in a few days they will begin again—these people are all leagued together, as soon as we go to one, they fly off to give notice to each other.
Prisoner's Defence. I am only a servant. I cannot say that I sold this book; I know I had two books stolen; it is my master's shop.
JAMES REEVES. I am a bookseller and news agent in Drury-lane. I am sent here by Mr. Blacketer, who is very ill in bed, to say that this person is only his servant, and that if anybody is liable it is himself—he wishes his servant to be cleared of this charge, as she has done it inadvertently—I have myself been six months in prison, not for selling prints, there was no sale, they could not produce the witness, he was gone to Dublin, but a party seized some in a friend's shop, where I happened to he at the time—I was not convicted of keeping a shop for the sale of such things—I was convicted, certainly, but there was no foundation for it—it was entirely a fabrication—I have a charge of conspiracy and felony against this Mr. Davis.
GUILTY. — Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18470816-1935]
1935. WILLIAM HUMPHREYS and JOHN PHEASANT were indicted for a conspiracy.
MESSRS. PAYNE and CAARTEEN conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM NICHOLSON, ESQ. I attend form the Crown-office of the Queen's Bench. I produce an indictment, preferred in the Queen's Bench against Thomas and Mary Twig, John and Sarah Wilson, and George and Mary Lane, for keeping a common brothel—the bill was found on the first count only—the witnesses on the back of the bill are John Pheasant and William Parsons—there is no other endorsement, therefore we assume that the witnesses are the prosecutors—I did not attend the Grand Jury on that occasion—I have the custody of the indictments pending there—warrants have been obtained, and the parties have appeared and pleaded—they applied for a summons, on account of no prosecutor's name appearing, and the whole proceeding being so extraordinary, and went before a Judge, who stayed the proceedings—( the summons was here handed in)—they could not give notice of trial in consequence of the not being able to serve the prosecutor—a prosecutor on an indictment of that sort does not enter into recognizance.
Humphreys. I served the notice on the attorney—we carried the indictment as far as possible, and Mr. Nicholson told me we could not do more till next Term. Witness. The hitch occurred when they could not serve notice of trial on the prosecutor.
WILLIAM EDWARD DREW. I come from the office of Clerk of the Peace for Middlesex—I produce an indictment, presented at the adjourned Session at Westminster, on the 16th of July—the names on the back of the bill are "John pheasant, of Bow-common, in the county of Middlesex, labourer," and "John Wormington"—I attended at that Session—I cannot tell whether Humphreys paid the fees on that occasion—I have no doubt of having seen him there—a Session seldom passes without my seeing him—I do not know Pheasant—the bill is against John Wooldridge, Frederick Annesley, Rose Joseph, Mary Smith, and Sarah Gale—there are two counts: one is for keeping a common bawdy-house—warrants have not been issued upon that—I believe some application was made to the Court—the course pursued by us is that the prosecutor shall enter into recognizance in 100 l. before the warrant is allowed to go out of the office, and he has not entered into that recognizance.
MARY LANE. I am the daughter of John Lane, and live at No.6, Lazcnbury-court. On the 24th of April, between eight and nine o'clock is the evening, Humphreys called at our house and asked if Mr. or Mrs. Lane were at home—they were not, and I saw him—he said he had called to inform my father and mother that they were under indictment, and that if we called on him at Mr. Delamere's office, 43, Lincoln's Inn-fields, we should receive every information, and that we hade nothing to fear—he said we were under indictment with two other houses of Twin's and Wilson's, and that he had been there and left his paper—I asked why he did not bring one to me, for I could not recollect all he had got to say, and I asked if he would be so kind as to put it down in writing—he said he would, and while I was getting him the pen, ink, and paper, I said, "I suppose you are aware that our house is not in the same parish; "I thought it was a parish concern—he turned round, looked at me, and told me not to ask him any more d—d questions; if I wanted further information I was to go to Mr. Delamere's office between eleven and twelve o'clock, or else go to the other houses and get information—I asked by whom we were indicted, and for what; and said we did not keep a disorderly house, or anything of the kind—he said it was Mr. Pheasant, of Bow, who had indicted us, but he could not tell us for what.
Humphreys. Q Will you swear that your's is not a disorderly house of the very worst description, where there are children of eleven or twelve years of age? A. I will—it is in the parish of St. Paul, Covent-garden—I am not to answer whether it is a common brothel—it is not an open house—we never let children in, neither do we keep any females.
COURT. Q. Did you ever see Pheasant at your house? A. Never, and I know every creature that enters it.
SARAH WILSON. I am the wife of John Wilson, and live at 29, Rose-street, Long-acre. On Saturday evening, the 24th of April, Humphreys called and left this paper with me, "W. H. Humphreys, at Mr. Delamere's 43, Lincoln's Inn-fields"—he said the house was under indictment, but if my husband would call on him on Monday morning, from eleven till twelve o'clock, things might be amicably arranged—he then said, "Good Night," and that was all—my husband does not keep any house jointly with Twig or Lane—he has nothing at all to do with it—they live at different places.
COURTS. Q. Do you know them? A. Only as neighbours—I am not aware of any ground for considering our house a brothel—he wished to extort money, and we did not think fit to give it him—ours is not a house that we were apprehensive of having indicted.
Humphreys. Q. Is it not a common brothel? A. No, it is a house of accommodation, but we do not let in children, as you with to insinuate.
COURT. Q. Did you ever see Pheasant at your house? A. No—I do not know him, nor can I say what means he can have of knowing how our house is conducted.
Pheasant. Q. The last time I was at your place, was I not robbed there? A. I never saw your face.
THOMAS TWIG. I am a brush-maker, and keep a coffee-house, at No.1, Lower Serle's-place, Temple-bar. On the 11th of June last, I was at West-minister-hall, and saw Humphreys there—understood that he was going to get some fresh bills of indictment—he asked me if I was coming to settle with him about the indictments, that if he had 10 l. from me, and 5 l. each from Wilson and Lane, he would squash the indictment, and if I did not come into those terms, he would ruin me—I told him I would not give him anything, for I did not keep a house in the parish—I did not keep or have a house in the parish at that time, nor had I done so for these four years—I keep no house jointly with Wilson and Lane, and never did, or ever partook in the profits of any house in which they were interested.
Humphreys. Q. How long is it since you were indicted by the parish of St. Martin's for no. 23, Rose-street, where you now live, and where you slept last night? A. Four years ago—my wife does not sit and take the money there—it is not a common brothel.
JOHN WORMINGTON. I know Humphreys, and have had conversations with him on this subject—he said there was a conspiracy, or an indictment, or something, against him—it was Pheasant that told me there was an indictment against these parties in Covent-garden—he said he was in trouble about an indictment against him, that he could scarcely show his face anywhere for fear of being taken up, and he said "Humphreys is in this trouble at the present time, he is confined at the Sessions-house by Serjeant Adams"—I said, "You are a stranger to me, I am let into something which I am not aware of, can you give me any explanation about this affair?"—he said, "Well, I don't know, it is a very strange thing altogether; will you go into the Sessions-house, and see what is the reason that Humphreys is not come out?"—that had reference to the indictment at the Session—about a week after that, I saw him by the Victoria, he asked me if I had seen Humphreys—I said I had not—he said "He has called on me"—I said, "Have you got paid for anything you have done—he said, "No, I have not"—I said, "I think he has led me and you into a shocking trouble"—he said, if I could arrange things in this matter, there was a party at Convent-garden who was indicted (he did not mention any name), and if I could go to that party, and get them to meet him, very likely he could remunerate me something—I did not do so—before I went before the Grand Jury at the Westminster Sessions, I saw Humphreys in the street, near the York Hotel, in the Waterloo-road—that was the first time I ever saw him—we came into communication, and he asked me some questions—he said, "Well, you know Davis?"—I said, "I have known the man as a neighbour for years"—after a little conversation he asked me if I knew Shepherd-street—I said, "Shepheard-street, where?"—he said, "Oxford-street"—I said, "I have known it for forty years, I lived near there"—he said, "Well, there is a chance of getting some money in Shepherd-street"—I said, "In what way?"—he said, "There is a man there who has offended me very much, named Wooldridge, and I mean to indict him"—I said, "I would rather not have anything to do with it"—he asked me to meet him on the
Friday morning, and said he should make a great deal of money of it, because before they would bring it into Court, they would pay him handsomely—he said he had no money then, but he would give me 2 l. or 3 l. after the conclusion of the affair—I afterwards met him, on the Friday morning, the 16th of July—we saw Pheasant, and went together to the Magpies public-house—Humphreys said to Pheasant, "Write some names out, you know what names to write out"—Pheasant wrote some names and gave them to me, I have not got the paper, I tore it up afterwards in vexation, but they were Wooldridge, Annesley, Joseph, Smith, and Gale—I asked where I was going to, and what was the meaning of it all, for I was not aware of it—he said, "Nor am I hardly aware what is going to be done"—I said, "You must be aware"—he said, "We are going into the Sessions Court"—I went in—Humphreys paid the fees, he said he was Mr. Delamere's clerk—Pheasant lives at No. 2, Star-court, Chancery-lane—he told me he never lived at Bow-common in his life—I have seen him at Star-court once—I went there by his direction—it was Humphreys' advice to say that he lived at Bow-common, upon the other indictment—that was what he mentioned to me at the Victoria when I parted with him.
Humphreys. Q. Did you see me pay any fees? A. Yes; to the clerk at the Court—I asked you to give me 1 s. or a trifle for my trouble, and you said you had to pay 15 s. at the Court—I did not state, before the Grand Jury, that No. 16, Shephered-street, was a common brothel—I said it was an accommodation-house, which it is, but I do not know the persons that live in it, or anything at all about it—I get my living by paying my way—I am not in every court of justice bailing people—I was in Horsemonger-lane for an unfortunate person who absconded from the country—I have had no money for coming here—I am to be paid for my trouble—I did not go to Twig, the other day, and demand money to pay my taxes, nor to Mrs. Gale, and have 1 l.—I never saw Mrs. Gale.
COURT. Q. Did you know that any of the persons Humphreys suggested had kept any of these houses? A. I did not—I was completely led into his—Pheasant went as a witness before the Grand Jury, before me.
Pheasant. Q. Did I ever have any conversation with you about the indictment at Westminster before that evening? A. No—I never saw you before I saw you at Westminster-bridge, when I saw Humphreys—the trouble you told me you were in, was a Court of Requests job—I will swear you told me about the indictment at Westminster—you said there was an indictment against you at the Queen's Bench, of a case at Covent-garden, and if I could go to Covent-garden, and see a party there, I could arrange the thing, and get a little money by it, but you could not go, and Humphreys could not go—I said I would have nothing at all to do with it, for I had had no sort of remuneration for what little time I had spent.
MR. PAYNE. Q. Was that Friday the first time you saw Pheasant? A. It was—whatever took place, was after that.
WILLIAM GIRDLER. I live on my income, at No.25, Queen-ssquare, West-minster—on Friday, the 16th of July, I happened to be at the Westminster Sessions on business of my own, and saw and heard a bill of indictment brought in—I cannot tell who it was against—I saw the prisoner Humphreys in Court—I knew nothing of any of these parties—Mr. Serjeant Adams said to the foreman of the Jury, "This is a very extraordinary bill, gentlemen"—the foreman said that it was, in the opinion of himself and his brother jurors—the Judge inquired who was the attorney in the case, and Humphreys stood up
and represented himself as the person—the Judge inquired the attorney's name—Humphreys said, "Mr. Delamere, of Lincoln's Inn-fields"—the Judge asked if he was Mr. Delamere—he said no, he was his clerk, that Mr. Delamere was generally to be found at the County Court, in Berner's-street, or at his chambers, 47, Lincoln's Inn-fields—the Judge then said, "This is a most extraordinary proceeding, that a gentleman should travel all the way from Bow-common; I should very much like to see the Bow-common Pheasant"—Humphreys said he was gone home—the Serjeant said, "No; he is not; you know where to find him; I dare say you could find him very soon if you liked"—he said, no; on his honour he was gone home—the Serjeant said that would not do, he must have a recognizance, or an affidavit, or something of that kind—I then told the Serjeant that I knew Mr. Delamere perfectly well, and I did not really think he had lent himself to a thing of that kind—Humphreys said that Mr. Delamere had instructed him in the prosecution, or words to that effect—I then went to Mr. Delamere, saw him, and returned to the Court, where Humphreys had been detained in custody—I told the Court, in Humphreys' hearing that Mr. Delamere knew nothing whatever of the prosecution, that it was an abuse of his name, that he begged of me to convince the Court that he was no cognizant of it, and that he would have come himself, but that he had three or four cases on, and could no leave the Court, and that Humphreys never was his clerk—the Serjeant said that he should not allow process to issue, and he would teach him a lesson for treating the Court with contempt, and he ordered him to be locked up—it was on the bail being brought in, that Humphreys said the Pheasant had gone away.
Humphreys. Q. Do you know Mr. Delamere's handwriting? A., No I do not think I do—I have known him for years, but I do not think I ever saw him write—( looking at a letter produced by Humphreys)—I do not know this to be his writing.
Humphreys. That letter would satisfy you that I am his clerk.
COURT. Q. I think he ought to be here—it concerns his own reputation—will you go for him? A. No, I will not—I have had great deal of trouble in this matter, and am brought here very much against my inclination—I was called upon by one of the parties who were prosecuted to ask if I would reduce the circumstances I had stated at the Sessions, into an affidavit—I said I would not do so without seeing Mr. Delamere—I saw Mr. Delamere, and he then desired me to repeat that Humphreys had been acting without his sanction, and was not his clerk—I made an affidavit of that, and the parties got it, and subpoenaed me.
HENRY CORNELIUS WRIGHT. I know Humphreys, and have paid him money on account of Mrs. Gale, one of the parties indicted, who has something to do with an accommodation-house—the first payment was on the 18th of Jan. this year—that was paid under the idea of suppressing the common informer's application, to keep him quiet—I paid him every month—the payments stopped in May, and the indictment followed immediately, in the ensuing month—I know Humphreys's handwriting—I received this letter from him—( read —"15, Warren-street, Dover-road, June 25. 1847.—Sir, —You may inform Mrs. Gale and Mrs. Patty, that if they are desirous to avoid trouble, they had better immediately close their houses in Shepherd-street.—Yours, &c., W. H. HUMPHREYS. ")
Humphreys. Q. Do you know anything of this letter? ( producing one.) A. Yes; it is my writing—( read —"To Mr. Humphreys.—Dear Sir, —I
have found you at last, after a little trouble. I went to No. 39, Great Suffolk-street, and the woman said she thought it was No.30, Warren-street. Now, Mrs. Gale wants to see you, without fail, to-morrow morning, at Oxendon-street, about eleven or twelve o'clock, to go with her on a little business for an hour or two. Please not to disappoint, and you will oblige, yours truly, H.C. WRIGHT.")—I wrote that letter by Mrs. Gale's direction—it was no doubt to see you on some subject with regard to the house—this other letter is my writing—( read —"Dear Sir, —I have this evening seen Wells, the sergeant here, who tells me that the Beech-street lawyer is at work again in the Common Pleas, and that to-morrow, one of his men, 78 or 87, is subpoenaed to attend at Westminster-hall, at nine o'clock. Please to look to this; the hint is enough to you, I know. Give us a call here soon; the parties named are Sarah Gale, Thomas Cornelius Wright, and Ann Gale")—That related to an indictment that was brought, or proposed to be brought, in the Queen's Bench—information of it was given me by Wells, and of course I looked up to you to protect Mrs. Gale.
COURT. Q. Do you know Pheasant? A. No, I never saw him, to my knowledge—I paid Humphreys a pound a month to protect Mrs. Gale against the innovations of common informers—he was to give any information to Mrs. Gale, and to prevent proceedings being taken against her.
MR. PAYNE. Q. He was always very friendly with her before this? A. Always, and when he ceased to be in her pay, he turned round and indicted her.
JOHN EDWARDS. I live with my father at No. 2, Star-court, Chancery-lane. I knew Pheasant by his lodging there ever since the 8th of May last—I never knew him of Bow-common.
Humphreys. Q. You do not mean to say that he slept there of night? A. I cannot say that—he generally lodged there.
Pheasant. I may have kept on my lodging there, as I have at Bow-common.
THOMAS WOOLDRIDGE. I am one of the persons indicted with Annealey, Gale, and others—I never kept, or was concerned in, a house jointly with them—I live at 16, Henry-street, Hampstead-road, in the parish of St. Pancras—I have no house in the parish of St. George, Hanover-square.
Humphreys Q. Will you swear you do not live at No. 16, Shepherd-street, Hanover-square? A. I do not—I have occasion to be there a good deal, as I was left guardian to two children, who have lost both their parents, and Mrs. Gunner, the woman who was left in charge of them, kept that house—she generally went by the name of Mrs. Harris—she died in Nov. last—she was not living when this indictment was preferred—Mary Smith was her housekeeper—she is not my housekeeper—Rose Joseph, is not concerned in that house to my knowledge—it belonged to a Mr. Joseph, I understand, but he is dead—he let it to Mrs. Harris for a certain time, which time is not yet up—I am not bound to say how much I have paid towards this prosecution—I have paid for my own counsel and attorney.
COURT. Q. Was any application ever made to you about this indictment? A. I met Humphreys at Clerkenwell, on Tuesday, the 3rd of Aug., and he called me a bawdy-house keeper, and said if I did not settle it with him, he would make it a warning to me—he said it to me in a low tone—I was not aware that I was indicted jointly with Mrs. Gale, who I know nothing at all of, until I saw it in the paper.
Humphreys. He certainly lives at the house; I never said what he represents; I indicted him and Mrs. Smith, for a society, about three years back,
at Clerkenwell, for keeping a house opposite Lincoln's-inn, and Mr. Payne was counsel in it. Witness. And you called for money previous to that, and sent a notice, stating that if the house was not shut, you would make it a warning, or threatened them in some way—I had nothing to do with it—I was locked up, and taken before Mr. Twyford, and Humphreys held up his fingers for three sovereigns, saying he would not ask for forty-eight hours' notice of bail—then he came down to two sovereigns—Mr. Twyford said he would not have notice at all, and he sneaked out of Court, and accepted the bail we had; and directly I got out I was indicted with two other parties—the clerk told Mr. Twyford that money was offered to forego it for forty-eight hours' notice of bail, and he said, "Do not pay any money at all"—I asked for you, and you sneaked out of Court, and I was dismissed directly.
MR. PAYNE. Q. He had included you in indictment with which you had nothing to do, and you were acquitted? A. Yes, it was proved that I had nothing to do with it, and I was sent out of court, and two others as well—Humphreys lives in the Kent-road.
Humphreys Q. Were you indicted by the parish for keeping No. 16, Shepherd-street? A. I was not, to my knowledge.
COURT. Q. Do you know Pheasant? A. I never saw him before to-day, to my knowledge—I have been to Bow-common, but could not find any person there who knew him.
JOHN LANE. I live at No.6, Lazenbury-court, Long-acre, in the parish of St. Paul, Covent-garden. I have also been indicted—I never kept a house in the parish of St. Martin, either by myself, or in partnership with Twig, Wilson, Gale, or King.
COURT. Q. Do you know anything of the person named Pheasant, who was a witness against you on that indictment? A. No, I have had a good deal of trouble in looking after him, but never could find him—Humphreys said he lived at Bow-common, near the cemetery—I went three times to Bow-common, but could find no such person there—I have seen Mr. Pheasant before, but I never knew that this was the Pheasant.
Humphreys' Defence. The prosecution was a bond fide one, and was carried on in the Queen's Bench in Mr. Delamere's name; I have here all the papers that were handed by Mr. Delamere to me to attend to the case; it has been duly attended to, and I defy any man to say that I ever received 1 s. from a brothel-keeper in my life, expect for some matter of business which they have requested me to do; Mr. Justice Coleridge and Mr. Justice Wightman asked Twig and the others, when they attended the summons, what they wanted to find the witness for; I said we never could get an indictment prosecuted, unless we kept the witness away, for if the defendants once get at them, there was no possibility of getting an indictment for trail; Mr. Justice Wightman made an order, but it was rescinded by another summons, taken out before Mr. Justice Coleridge; when the two Judges met in the robbing-room at Westminster-hall, and Atkinson and Twig appeared before them, Mr. Justice Coleridge said, "Brother Wightman, what can they want to find this witness for? it appears to me that I have now discovered why they don't come to trail; these brothel-keepers get at the witnesses and tamper with them, and it is impossible to get an indictment to trail," and such is the fact; one witness was hunted out of three references, and was obliged to consent to abandon the prosecution before he could get rid of the annoyance of these parties; as far as relates to Shepherd-street, the parish has been to 500 l. expense in prosecuting them, but they have kept up the brothels in defiance of them up to this time; I was applied to, and I asked this person and the other to go
into the house to prove the fact, before ever there was an indictment; the inhabitants there, told me they were afraid to come forward again; for on the last occasion they did so, people were set to break their windows, and their property was destroyed by persons whom they did not know, in the absence of the police; I offered to Mr. Serjeant Adams to enter into recongnizance and give security to any amount to prosecute the case, so that I could keep the witness away from the defendants, which he refused, and said he would commit me for contempt of Court, in practising as an attorney without authority; I said I was not; what could I do? if I was to be treated in that way I could never get to trail; if they can get me convicted, and kept out of the way, there is an end of the 480 l. worth of recognizance that are now estreated in the Queen's Bench; I applied to Mr. Nicholson the other day; he said, "You cannot try the case till next term;" I said, according to practice, I thought we could; the first summons was taken out before Mr. Justice Wightman, who made an order; we complied with that order; a second summons was taken out before Mr. Justice Coleridge, for a better description of the prosecutor; I attended before him, and stated the case; he saw Mr. Justice Wightman upon it, rescinded the order that Mr. Justice Wightman had made, and fixed the recognizance, and it is to get rid of the recognizances that they have placed me here; the law allows three or four houses to be included in one indictment; it is so laid down in "Dixon's Practice;" these people are all backwards and forwards in one another's houses, and we cannot tell which is which; there never was such a batch of perjury as there is here; as for the money I received from Mrs. Gale, it did not amount to 1 s. a mile for distance they sent me.
Pheasant's Defence. I was passing along Long-Acre one evening, and was taken into this brothel, and I was not only taken into one place, but into two, where they gave me something to drink drugged it, and took 18 s. or 19 s. out of my pocket; I was determined to indict the house, and I did so, and I determined to follow it up; this is the first time I have heard anything about any offers having been made; I know nothing whatever of that; if anything of that kind has been done, it has been without my sanction or knowledge.
HUMPHERYS — GUILTY. Aged 54.— Fined 50 l., Confined Eighteen Months, and to enter into his own Recognizance in 200 l. for five Years.
PHEASANT — GUILTY. Aged 38.— Confined One Year, and to enter into his own Recognizance in 40 l. for Three Years.
[trialAccount t18470816-1936]
1936. JAMES JOHNSON, alias Wilkins , GEORGE PHILLIPS, alias Lamb , and SAMUEL BARKER were indicted for stealing 1 half-crown; the monies of Robert Thackeray, from the person of Theresa Thackeray; and that Phillips and Barker had been before convicted of felony.
MR. RYLAND conducted the Prosecution. THERSEA THACKERAY. I am the wife of Robert Thackery, of James-place, George-street, King's-cross. On Sunday morning, the 1st of Aug.; about ten minutes to ten, I was in Chapel-street, Somers'-town—I had halfa-crown and a halfpenny in my pocket—the witness Parsons spoke to me—I searched my pocket, and the half-crown was gone—the half-penny remained—the three prisoners were pointed out to me by Parsons, who told me that one of them had picked my pointed—I tried to lay hold of the one that he said had the half-crown—I caught hold of Johnson and another—I cannot say which picked my pocket—they got away from me, and all three ran—there
was a cry of "Stop thief!" and Johnson was taken first—he said if I waited a minute he would give me the money back.
Johnson. Q. Did you not first say to me, that if I gave you the half-crown back you would let me go? A. Yes; you said you would give me the half-crown if I waited, and of course you would not do that unless you had taken it.
Cross-examined by MR. ROBINSON. Q. There are always a good many persons about there on Sunday morning? A. Yes, there is a sort of market there—they walked steadily up the street as far as the length of this Court, till I took hold of two of them—I had them in sight all the way, and then they ran
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Barker was not in custody the first time you went before the Magistrate? A. No, he was taken afterwards—Parsons did not point him out to me as the person who had taken my half-crown—he was one of the three—two of them were before the Magistrate on Monday, the other I think on Friday—Parsons pointed out the three to me, and told me one of them had the half-crown—when I caught hold of two of them, they were all three standing in a row
JOHN PARSONS. I am a shoemaker, at 12, Ossulton-street, St. Pancras. On Sunday morning, the 1st of Aug., I was in Chapel-street, Somers'-town, and saw Mrs. Thackeray and the three prisoners there—they were close together—I saw Barker put his hand into her pocket, and take out a half-crown-piece, the other two were close behind him at the time and must have seen him do it—they were close alongside, as if pushing him up—I saw the half-crown in his hand when he drew it out—they then left the place, and went to the middle of the road—Barker spun the half-crown up, and said she had not got above 6 d. left—I then went and asked Mrs. Thackeray if she had lost half-a-crown, she felt in her pocket, and missed it—I did not attempt to take either of them into custody—I saw Mrs. Thackeray attempt it, but they got away.
Cross-examined by MR. ROBINSON. Q. How near you to Barker when he tossed up the half-crown? A. Closer than I am to you—I distinctly heard him say those words—the half-crown did not go up very high—I was immediately behind him, but I could see it in his hand as he went into the road—there were a great number of persons there—the street is not very wide or very narrow.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Who do you work for? A. Mr. Leach; I worked for him last week, and have done so for nine years—on this Sunday morning I had been to get some provisions—I have never been a witness before—I am not an amateur policeman, nothing of the kind; I get my living by industry—the reason I did not assist the prosecutrix to take the prisoners, was that my arms were full of provisions at the time—I was at regular work the whole of the week before.
JAMES LIPPETT. I am a shoemaker, and live at 46, Wilstead-street, Somers'-town. On Sunday morning, the 1st of Aug., I was standing at my door, heard a cry of "Stop thief"!" and saw John running—I stopped him—Mrs. Thackeray came up soon after, and said he had stolen a half-crown from her—he said nothing to that—I took him into custody—he said on the road to the station, that if I would let him go he would get the woman the half-crown—I did not think that advisable.
GEORGE MANLEY ( policeman, S 311.) In consequence of information, I apprehended Phillips, at eleven o'clock the same day, in Chapel-street—I told him the charge—he said it was a b—y lie, he had not robbed the woman.
Cross-examined by MR. ROBINSON Q. You took him very much about the same spot, did you not? A. I do not know whether it was near the spot; it was in Chapel-street—I do not know where he lives—there is something remarkable about his eyes—I have heard that he is nearly blind.
HENRY TOVEY ( policeman, S 231.) In consequence of information, I apprehend Barker, on the 6th of Aug.—I found him in the back-parlour of No.2, Paradise-street—I do not know whether it was his lodging—I told him he was wanted, for picking a pocket of a half-crown-piece—he said, "So help me G—, if you had been another minute later you would have lost me"—the parlour-window was up at the time.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did not he say, "If you had been a minute later you would not have found me?" A. No, I am sure he made use of the expression, "So help me G—."
Johnson's Defence. I am entirely innocent, though appearances are against me; I was going up Wilstead-street, when I was stopped and charged by the prosecutrix with robbing her of half-a-crown; she said, if I gave it her back she would not prosecute me; I denied the charge, but being excited at the time I said I would give her one rather than be locked up, as I was going to work next morning; I never saw the half-crown, neither was I in company with either of the prisoners
JOHN HARDY ( policeman S 205.) I produce a certificate of Phillips' former convietion—( read—George Lamb, convicted Feb., 1844, and confined three months)—I was present at the trail—he is the person.
JEREMIAH LOCKABY ( policeman S 180.) I produce a certificate of Barker's conviction—( read — Convicted Aug., 1845, and confined four months)—he is the person.
JOHNSON — GUILTY. ** Aged 19.
PHILLIPS — GUILTY. ** Aged 20.
BARKER — GUILTY. ** Aged 18.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18470816-1937]
1937. JOHN PENNY was indicted for stealing 12 feet of lead-pipe, value 10 s.; the property of William Witley Coorze, his master; and RICHARD GARRETT for feloniously receiving the same, knowing it to have been stolen.
JOHN RICKETTS. I am foreman, in the employ of William Witley Coorze, of 20, Frith-street, Soho—Penny was in his employ for four years—he was at work there on the 16th of Aug.—he generally had on his working dress, a light jacket and cap—there was a piece of lead-pipe, weighing about fifty-four pounds, on the premises—it was safe on Saturday night.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. You did not see it since the Saturday previous? A. It was a piece resembling this ( produced) in all respects—I know the weight of the piece I lost, this answers in point of weight—I have not weighed it since—the policeman has—it has not been in my custody—we had no reason to believe otherwise than that Penny bore a good character up to this time—we did not miss the lead till the constable came and made enquiries.
EZER FREEMAN ( policeman, C 178.) I was in Prince's street, at a quarter before one o'clock in the afternoon of the 16th of Aug., and saw the prisoner Penny standing at the corner of George-court, in Prince's street—he had a light jacket and a cap cn, and this leaden pipe, which I have produced, was standing by his side—I watched him, and after a short time I saw the prisoner Garrett come up to him—the lead pipe was put on his shoulder, and
he walked up George-court into Rupert-street with it—Penny walked down another way, in a contrary direction, which caused my suspicion—I saw a horse and cart standing in Upper Rupert-street, where Garrett keeps a marine store shop—he came up Rupert-street, down Richmond-street, into Rupert-street again, and the lead pipe was put into the cart, which was standing nearly opposite his own house—I asked him where he had got it from—he said he had brought it out of his own cellar—I asked whether he had brought it direct from his cellar to the cart—he said, "Yes," and he would take me down stairs and show me where he had brought it from, that he had a cistern there—I went down with him, he had a cistern there—I told him I should take him into custody, on suspicion of the lead being stolen—he said it was all right, would I have a drop of something to drink—I refused, and said I should take him to the station-house, and charge him.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Had you know Penny at all? A. No—I swear he is the person, because I took such particular notice of him—I do not go from his dress in particular, but from his appearance and features—the place I saw him at, was not a quarter of a mile from the prosecutor's.
COURT. Q. Have you weighted the lead? A. Yes, it weights fifty-four pounds.
Cross-examined by MR. METCALF. Q. What does that purport to be ( handing a paper to the witness)? A. "Bought of T.A. Hockley, one quarter and 22lbs."—I know nothing of Hockley—the weight here does not correspond with the lead I found, it is four pounds lighter—the date corresponds—I went into Garrett's cellar, and saw a cistern in Garrett's cellar—whether it was being built I cannot say, or whether leaden pipe of this sort would be used in making it—he said, before the Magistrate, that the leader pipe was to fit a pipe that was in his cistern; but that pipe has never been cut—when I first saw the lead, it was standing by Penny—I cannot say where Garrett came from—I first saw him put the lead on his shoulder—I am sure he was not there before—I was looking round the corner, and I saw the lead put on his shoulder, and saw him walk away with it—I had then been there five minutes—I cannot see any mark on the lead.
COURT. Q. Have you any doubt about Penny being the same man? A. None at all, and I followed Garrett to his very door—I found Penny at work, at a job in Regent-street, for his master, Mr. Coorze.
MR. PAYNE to JOHN RICKETTS. Q. Did you not say this weighed about fifty pounds? A. Fifty-four pounds—I said before the Magistrate that it was fifty pounds, because I did not wish to convict the man, and I hope the Court will deal leniently with him, he has a wife and three children—I had the weight of the lead chalked down tow or three days previously, I weighed it myself, as there was to be an estimate sent in.
PENNY — GUILTY. Aged 30.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury. — Confined Three Months
GARRETT — GUILTY. Aged 27.— Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18470816-1938]
1938. SARAH BROOKS was indicted for stealing 1 needles-case, value 1 s.; 1 pincushion, 1 s.; 1 necklace, 5 s.; 1 scent-bottle, 1 s.; 2 habit-shirts, 2 s.; 5 packets of needles, 1 s.; 1 pair of boots, 6 d.; 1 skipping-rope, 3 s.; 1 bunch of artificial flowers, 6 s.; 1 toy, 3 d.; 1 brooch, 1 s.; and 1 necklace, 6 d.; the goods of Charlotte Sophia Clifton, her mistress.
CHARLOTTE SOPHIA CLIFTON. I live at No. 4, Berkeley-place, Connaught-square—the prisoner was in my service for one month, and was my only servant—she was to leave on Saturday night, the 2nd of Aug.—having some suspicion of her, I insisted on searching her box that morning, which was out-side the kitchen door—there were three or four witnesses present at the time it was searched—the prisoner gave me the key out of her pocket, and I unlocked the box—it was a little key with a chain to it—I found in the box a pair of French stays, a pair of new stockings, two pocket-handkerchiefs, two books, and a scent-bottle, which I said were mine—I did not find a needle-case there, that was found at her sister's lodgings, which the officer searched—her sister never visited her at my house—I had a lady visiting me at the time.
Cross-examined by MR. METCALYF. Q. What were the prisoner's wages to be? A. Eight pounds a year—she was to have nothing besides wages—I never gave her things, she was too short a time in my service for that—I was constantly missing things—I am a single woman—there are no other persons living in my house, only a professional lady—she is no relation of mine, but a friend—I received a character with the prisoner—there stays cost me two guineas, they are quite new, and were going to be cleaned—I am quite independent—I was induced to look into her box, because I had missed a sovereign in a very singular manner—I changed a 5 l. note, and had it all in gold, I never went out after receiving it, and I dropped a sovereign, and asked the prisoner if she had seen a sovereign about, she said, "No"—I searched thoroughly that night, but could not find it; and the next day I said I was very uneasy about it, I must have it found, we came into the parlour together, I would not allow anybody else to sweep the room, and underneath the sofa, where we had looked the night before, I found the sovereign placed—I had been on the sofa the night before, I had dropped it in the parlour—it could not have rolled where I found it—the lady that was with me is here—the prisoner is not charged with stealing the sovereign, but I suspected her of many things—I told her to leave, but said I must search her box before she went out—I had a great many ladies in the room, and we all looked underneath the sofa for the sovereign—there were none but ladies there that night—there were no gentlemen—one lady was my sister—perhaps the others might not like their names mentioned—Mrs. Hawkes, my niece, my sister, and myself, were all in the room the night before, there was not a nook or corner in which we did not search—I had given the prisoner a month's warning—she was not servant enough, and I suspected her—I was constantly losing things out of my dressing-room, ever since she has been with me, and when I found this property in her box, I asked the lady to go up stair and see if she missed anything, she came down, and said she had lost three large sheets—the prisoner did not give me warning, warning was given before the sovereign was lost—her duties were merely to keep the house clean, the kitchen, and the upper part of the house—she did not have to sit up late—I always made her go to bed—if I was out late, I always let myself in, she was not to sit up—I never keep company—she was not required to sit up late the night before.
RICHARD COMPTON ( policeman, D 87.) On Saturday afternoon, at half-past two o'clock, I took the prisoner in custody, at No. 4, Berkeley-place—I did not see the box searched—the same evening, between four and five o'clock, I went to N. 10, George-street, and saw a person there who represented herself to be the prisoner's sister—she showed me a box which she said was hers, and
in it I found five packets of needles, a pincushion, and necklace, which I produce—I found nothing on the prisoner's person—the witness, who did find something on her is not here—another officer found some boots and a skipping-rope, at another place.
Cross-examined. Q. Is Berkeley-place in your beat? A. No; it is in the same division—I know nothing about the house where this happened—I found the articles I have produced in the house of Susan Brooks.
SUSAN BROOKS. I am the prisoner's sister—I know these articles that have been produced—the prisoner brought them to me last Monday fortnight, in the state they are now in—I had this necklace, a smelling-bottle, and a pincushion, all of the prisoner at the same time—I had no others.
Cross-examined. Q. Were you taken into custody on this charge? A. Yes; I was remanded first, and then released by the Magistrate—my brother was also taken.
COURT. Q. Do you live with your mother and father? A. We have a mother but no father—I do not live with her—I was living at my situation—the things were found where I was living as servant—they were all delivered to me at one time, on the Monday.
MR. METCALFE. Q. Where was the prisoner living before this? A. At Cumberland-market, I think—I do not know whether she was there long—she bore a very good character—I never heard anything against her till this—she is nineteen years old, I believe.
COURT. Q. Were these things given to you as a present? A. Yes; she gave me a parcel at the same time, to deliver to my brother—I did not open it.
EDWARD BROOKS. I live at No. 8, Walmer-street, New-road. The last witness and the prisoner are my sisters—the last witness gave me a parcel to give to my sister, as she was going home to see her mother and brothers on the Saturday morning—I gave West the parcel—I did not know what it contained till he brought it to the station; it was in a torn piece of rag.
BENJAMIN WEST ( policeman.) These things were delivered to me by Edward Brooks, out of a box in the back room of his lodging—they were tied up in an apron; when we untied them, the prosecutrix owned them—I did not bring the apron away with me, therefore the parcel is not in the state in which I found it; the outside cover is off.
MR. METCALF to EDWARD BROOKS. Q. Do you know anything of the prosecutrix's house? A. Yes; I believe it is a common brothel—I went there, on the 21st of July, to see my sister, and when the door was opened a lady came down stairs tipsy, and my sister told me that it was her mistress, and that was is general the case, as she never knew her to go to bed till three or four o'clock in the morning—I should know that lady—I believe it was the prosecutrix.
COURT. Q. What time was it? A. Between seven and eight in the evening—my sister told me it was her mistress—I saw her go across the passage into the room, and she could hardly walk—that is not the only reason I have for thinking it is a brothel—my sister told me when it was time to go to bed, that her mistress would wish her to go to bed on the sofa, and in the presence of a lot of men—she had complained to me before, and told me she must give her mistress warning; and that her mistress said if she left she would give her no money, and threatened to throw her out into the street—that was what my sister said—before she went to this house she had been living eighteen months in the country—I believe she has always borne a good character up to this present time.
CHARLOTTE SOPHIA CLIFTON re-examined. This needle-case is mine—I know it, because I bought it myself—there is no mark on it; it was in my dressing-room—I have a person to come and work and mend my clothes—this necklace is mine, it was given me as a present, it is made of Roman pearls—I should think it was worth half-a-guinea, that was what the lady told me when she gave it me—there is no gold about it, it is a peculiar sort of necklace—it was on my dressing-table; she must have taken it off my dressing-table, from one of my china-jars—this scent-bottle I know; I have had it a long time, and the pincushion—I never gave them to the prisoner—they were not found in her trunk—the prisoner was dressed very neatly, and was very clean in her person while in my service—I did not supply her with clothes or ornaments of any sort—I got her character form a friend of hers, living at West Drayton—she was never in service before—I did not ask her age—my reason for parting with her was not on account of the dispute about the sovereign—she was going to leave me on the Saturday night; her month was up—she had not given me warning, she said she was not going into service any more—I had told her long before, that she was not servant enough for me; she could not keep the house clean
MR. METCALF. Q. Who is the person whom you said you had to work for you—I understood you just now that you had no one in the house but the prisoner and a lady? A. She was not there on the Saturday; she was there on the Tuesday, to mend the clothes—she has been in the habit of coming there ever since I have been in the house, which is fourteen years—I have very few ladies come to visit me, and I never receive gentlemen, not one—the prisoner's bed-room was down in the kitchen, but as the lady had her lady's maid she said she would lay on the sofa, in the front parlour, I was in the back—the lady that was with me had the second-floor, and I had the ground-floor—there is no bed-room on the drawing-room floor—there are two drawing-rooms—I was present sometimes when the prisoner went to bed, no one else was present—she has never had to go to bed in the presence of any one besides me—when I went to bed, I saw her on the sofa—if I was at home, we went to bed together—she has never had to go to bed in any one else's presence, unless it was my sister's; she was not asked to go to bed in any one else's presence—we were alone—she never went to bed, if any one was in the room—we were quite alone on Friday night—if I went out, she went to bed, and I let myself in—the ladies I had with me, just came to stop for an hour, and went away again—when I went out and let myself in, perhaps I have gone to a party, or to the theatre, with ladies; I do not require gentlemen when I go out at that time of night—we go to the theatre in the evening, and come home in proper time, without any gentlemen—the time I come home, depends upon where I go to—if I am at a ball I come home at one or two o'clock—I do not often go to balls—I do not think I have been at theatres once a month.
Q. Who is the professional lady living in the house? A. Miss Lynes, an actress—no one visits her at the house—there were no other visitors in the house—when my sister slept there, she slept with me, and the servant slept in the front parlour on the sofa—I had not room for her elsewhere, as Miss Lynes had a lady's maid who took the servant's bed—no gentlemen ever came to the house—I cannot say how long it is since a gentleman has been in the house, I do not think it is necessary to answer that—I give no parties at home—I never receive any company—I have had no gentleman there since the prisoner has been with me—no gentleman has visited the house since the prisoner has been there—I do not know whether any of the ladies
who visit at my house are fond of wine—I shall not answer whether I am fond of a little port wine—I have never indulged in too much wine or too much liquor of any sort.
COURT. Q. Do you remember the prisoner's brother calling one evening? A. Never—she asked me whether I would allow her brother to come and see her, but I said, "I am very sorry to be obliged to refuse you, as I never allow followers"—he did not come in while I was crossing the passage—I never saw him in my house—my sister's name is Augusta Bradley.
AUGUSTA BRADLEY. I am the sister of the last witness, and was in the house at the time the prisoner was there—I recollect her box being searched—there had been a dispute between her and my sister about a sovereign, but she was going to leave my sister on the Saturday evening—my sister was going out in the morning, and thought it best to discharge her before she left the house—I was going to remain.
Cross-examined. Q. Where did the prisoner sleep? A. Sometimes on the sofa, in the front room—her time of going to bed was sometimes early, sometimes late—she very often had to sit up for us, sometimes till twelve o'clock and sometimes later, but very seldom—it was very rarely that she set up till three in the morning—now and then she did—she was not long with us—we had no company that evening, only my sister's lodger—I was not at the house there on the Friday evening, at the loss of the sovereign, but I came in in the morning, and my sister told me of it—we have not many visitors at this house, only my sister's family, and now and then a female friend, but that very seldom: no gentlemen—I do not remember seeing a gentleman there, for my sister lives very quietly—I do not remember seeing a gentleman there while the prisoner was there—I do not remember how long it is since any gentleman was there—I have not seen any gentleman, only a friend now and then, a particular friend of ours, for my sister does not keep company—I and my sister very often go out—the servant sits up for us sometimes—we never asked the prisoner to go to bed on the sofa in the presence of anybody—she went to bed when my sister did—she always slept on the sofa in the parlour, I believe.
COURT. Q. Did any gentleman ever come to see your sister home when she had been out? A. No, not to my knowledge—when I was at my sister's I always used to sleep with her—there were no gentlemen there of an evening when the prisoner was there, either in the drawing-room or any other room—I do not remember whether any gentleman was there on the Thursday—I was very seldom there.
MR. METCALF called
LYDIA BROOKS. I am married, and am sister-in-law to the prisoner. I know this house where my sister was servant—I have been there frequently—I went with her when she took the place—a fortnight after that, she complained, and said that her mistress wished her to go to bed on the sofa, where there were several gentlemen in the room—she told me that the next day, and I wished her to leave her situation—she said she would give her warning—I did not take her away at once, because I was not aware what sort of a place it was—I did not know whether she could leave—her mistress said her wages would be stopped if she left—the prosecutrix has sworn to a brooch of mine, as I have witnesses to prove—the prisoner has borne a very good character.
COURT. Q. Was there anything to create your suspicion when you went with the prisoner? A. No; only I saw the mistress several times in her might-gown—every time I went there I have seen her so, in the afternoon
part of the day, about three o'clock, all about the house, and in the drawing-room—it was a dressing-gown, similar to a night-gown—I do not know whether there was any clothing underneath—I have not seen any men there, but I have seen several different men coming while the brother and sister were lying for trial—I saw many different faces, three or four—they came with them to High-street, and to the station-house.
CHARLOTTE SOPHIA CLIFTON re-examined. This woman never came into the house all, but she is the woman who gave me the character of the prisoner; and as to gentlemen, I have never had any gentleman in my house, I think, for these six months—I never came home at three o'clock—there is not a more domesticated lady than myself—every word she has said is false—that girl has never seen a gentleman in my house since she has been in my service, and I have numbers of witnesses to prove that I live respectably, I have lived in the house fourteen years—I wear a dressing-gown in the morning, because I see to my household affairs before I dress.
( GEORGE EDWARD HORSLEY, of West Drayton, stated that the prisoner lived eighteen months in his mother's service, and bore a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 19.
[trialAccount t18470816-1939]
1939. SARAH BROOKS was again indicted for stealing 1 pair of stays, value 25 s.; 1 night-gown, 5 s.; 1 pinafore, 1 s.; 1 pair of stockings, 5 s.; 1 night-cap, 2 s.; 1 neck-tie, 6 d.; 1 skein of silk, 6 d.; 2 handkerchiefs, 2 s.; 2 printed books, 10 d.; and 1/4 lb. weight of soap, 2 d.; the goods of Charlotte Sophia Clifton, her mistress.
CHARLOTTE SOPHIA CLIFTON. When the prisoner's box was searched, she gave me the key to open it—I found in the box a pair of stays, a new night-gown, a nightcap, a pincloth, a pair of stockings, a neck-tie, two books, and two pocket-handkerchiefs—I have a set of the books—they are all my property—the maker's name is on the stays, on a printed card, where I bought them—the prisoner had not the charge of my things—she did not keep her box in the room we both slept in, but down stairs—I leave my things in my dressing-room—she could not have put the things into her box, out of the way—I had put the stays into a basket, to be unburned, to be prepared for cleaning—the night-gown was in the wardrobe up stairs, that was unlocked—the stockings were put into a basket, to be ready for mending—she might have got the books out of my parlour, where I slept—the stays I gave two guiness for, but I value them at 1 l. 5 s.
Cross-examined by MR. METCALF. Q. Was her box kept locked or unlocked? A. Always locked, I have the key at home—she gave it me out of her pocket—it was her own trunk—she trembled very much when I opened her box, and I said, "You no need to tremble, Sarah, if you are honest"—I took the property out before several witnesses—there of them are here now.
LOUISA FELLOWERS HARDY. I was not present when the prisoner's box was opened, but I was when the things were found—I was there on visit, and I went down stairs in the morning, to bid Mrs. Clifton good-bye, and found her and Mrs. Bradley searching the box—I saw the things all taken out of the box.
Cross-examined. Q. Why do you call her Mrs. Clifton? A. I know the lady by that name—I believe she is married—I have not known her long enough to tell you that.
COURT. Q. Were you on a visit? A. Yes, I am no relation of hers, I am married—my husband was not with me, he is in the country—I have
known Mrs. Clifton four or five months, I cannot say precisely—I became acquainted with her in town—I think I had been on a visit there on day—I was there the whole of one day, and slept there during the night, and in the morning I went into the kitchen to bid her good—bye, and saw the two sisters searching the box—I had slept with Mrs. Clifton that night, and the servant slept on the sofa—I had never known the lady by any other name than Mrs. Clifton—I have not the slightest reason to suppose she is married.
Prisoner. The articles were not found in the box.
CHARLOTTE SOPHIA CLIFTON ( re-examined.) Certainly they were, and I heave tow more witnesses that saw them—they were rolled up in a handkerchief in the box—I took them out myself.
GUILTY. Aged 19.— Judgment respited.

[trialAccount t18470816-1942]
1942. ELIZABETH SIMTH was indicted for a misdemeanour.
FREDERICK WILLIAM HAMMOND. I am the son of Francis Manesty Hammond, of the Churchill-arms, Kensington. On the 18th of August, at half-past six o'clock, the prisoner came and left a bottle, and said she would call for it in a few minutes—she came again, and wanted it filled with vinegar, and wanted a bottle of the best rum, for Mr. Smith, of Bedford-place—I know him, he deals with us regularly—I gave the prisoner the rum and vinegar, believing she came from Dr. Smith—she went away—I watched her up Bedford-place—she did not go to Dr. Smith's, but turned into the New-road—I stopped her, and told her that was not the way to Dr. Smith's—she said she was going to call as she came back—she put the rum and vinegar on the path, and said if I did not believe her I might take them myself, and was going to walk away—I said she should not go, unless she went with me—this is the rum and vinegar—( produced.)
THOMAS BROWNING SMITH. I live in Bedford-place, Kensington Graavepits, and deal with Mr. Hammond, of the Churchill-arms. I never sent the prisoner there for any rum or vinegar—she had never been in my service—I had never seen her before.
SAMUEL PUDDEFOOT ( policeman.) I was present when the prisoner was taken, and received the rum and vinegar—the prisoner said she was sorry for what she had done; she knew it was quite wrong.
GUILTY. Aged 27.— Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18470816-1943]
1943. HENRY SAMUEL FERGUSON was indicted for unlawfully assaulting ANN Elizabeth Cresswell, a girl under ten years of age, with intent, &c. 3rd COUNT, for a common assault.

[trialAccount t18470816-1944]
1944. SARAH TERRY, the younger , was indicted for stealing 1 pair of trowsers, value 11 s.; the goods of Samuel Lyons, her master; and SARAH TERRY , for feloniously receiving the same, knowing them to be stolen.
SAMUEL LYONS. I am a tailor, and live in Tothill-fields, Westminster. The younger prisoner was in my service up to Thursday evening, the 29th of July, about seven o'clock, when she left without notice—she wanted to take a gown to her mother to be altered, and I let her go—I missed a pair of trowsers about eight o'clock, off a gas-branch over the window—I had seen them all that week—these produced are them—they are new—they are mine—there is no mark on them—there is stain inside the pocket, on a piece of plaid—they are my own work—they were bespoke, but they were not called for in time—no one assists in my shop—my wife was at Gravesend at the time—the prisoner lived with me ten days—I had a character with her.
DAVID CHAMBERS. I am servant to Mr. Carter, pawnbroker, of Regent-street. I produce a pair of trowsers, pledged on the 29th of July, at a little before eight o'clock, by the elder prisoner—I am quite certain of her—I have known her seven or eight years—she said, "David, these are not mine, they belong to Mrs. Mansfield; put them in Mrs. Mansfield's name"—Mrs. Carter wrote the ticket in that name—the younger prisoner was at the door.
WILLIAM MONTAGUE ( policeman, B 179.) On the 30th of July I took the elder prisoner into custody—she asked me if her daughter was found; I told her, "No"—I took her to Mr. Carter's, and Chambers saw her—as I was taking her back to the station, she said to Mr. Lyons, "Do not say any-thing about them, you will have them all right by twelve o'clock"—she said, "The law can take no hold of me, I only took the parcel into the shop; it was pawned by another woman"—On the 4th of August I took the younger prisoner, on the Surrey-side of Westminster-bridge—I took her to the station, said she was charged with stealing trowsers, and that she had got her mother into trouble—she said, "Serve her right; if I stole it, she had no right to pledge it, and I hope to God she will get two years for it."
Sarah Terry's Defence. My daughter brought the trowsers, and told me to get 8 s. on them, in the name of Mansfield; I knew she was keeping company with a young man, and thought he had given them her; I said, "Are you sure you are telling me the truth?" she said, "Yes."
Sarah Terry the younger's Defence. I took them but with the intention of replacing them—any mother innocent.
The prisoner called
CHARLOTTE ---- I am single—I have known both prisoners about twelve months, and never knew anything against their honesty—I was at the house when the younger prisoner brought the trowsers home, and asked her mother to pledge them
(the prisoner received good character.)
SARAH TERRY, the younger— GUILTY. Aged 17.— Confined Two Months.
SARAH TERRY — NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18470816-1945]
1945. SARAH BARTON was indicted for stealing 2 blankets, value 10 s.; the goods of John Wright, her master.
ELIZABETH WRIGHT. I am the wife of John Wright, a stationer. The prisoner left our service on the 31st of July—she lived there five months—on the day she left, I missed some blankets—these produced are them—they are mine, and were on the servant's bed before they were lost—when the prisoner was taken I saw her take two duplicates from her pocket.
GEORGE YOUNG. I am shopman to Mr. Dulne, pawnbroker of the Strand. On the 9th of last July a blanket was pledged for 1 s. 6 d., and another on the 17th, for 1 s.—these are them—I gave the person duplicates—these duplicates produced are the corresponding ones.
---- ----( policeman.) I produce two duplicates—the prisoner produced one at Mr. Wright's, the other was found on her at the station.
WILLIAM POCOK ( policeman.) I took the prisoner, and received two duplicates, one from the prisoner, and the other was found on her.
GUILTY. Aged 53.— Recommended to mercy by the prosecutor. — Confined Three Months

1947. ALEXANDER MACDONALD was indicted for stealing 4 dies, value 20 s. and 7 taps, 10 s.; the goods of William Mark, his master.
WILLIAM MARK. I am a smith, and live in George-yard, Golden-lane. On the 7th of Aug. I employed the prisoner to work for me—he went away without finishing his work—on the 8th of Aug. I missed four dies and seven taps from a bureau—the prisoner could get to where they were kept—they were safe on the 6th—I have not seen them since—when the prisoner was given in charge he had these two taps ( produced) in his hand—they are mine.
WILLIAM CONSTABLE ( policeman.) I took the prisoner—he had two taps—he told me he took them from Mr. Mark's premises, and sold the dies to a man for 1 s.
Prisoner's Defence. I was very badly off, and took them.
GUILTY. Aged 23.— Confined Three Months.

[trialAccount t18470816-1949]
1949. JOSEPH REED was indicted for embezzlement.
JOHN MARTIN. I am a cheesemonger, at St. Marylebone—the prisoner was my errand-boy—it was his duty to receive money, and account to me for it—he was with me nearly twelve months—he left about the 8th or 10th of July—he had been with me twelve months before, and I hired him on this occasion in the absence of a boy who had a holiday—he never accounted to me for any money received of Eleanor Ann Cridge, on my account, from Colonel West.
Prisoner. I know I paid my master.
ELEANOR ANN CRIDGE. I live with Colonel West, in Aberdeen-place, Maida-hill.
Mr. Martin supplies us with butter and eggs—On the 3rd of August the prisoner came with some butter and eggs—he brought this bill, for 1 l. 3 s. 8 d. ( produced), I saw him write a receipt to it—I paid him a sovereign, a half-crown, two sixpences, and twopence.
PAUL JONES ( policeman.) On Saturday night, about half-past nine o'clock, I met Mr. Martin with the prisoner—he gave him in charge—I told the prisoner what he was charged with—he said he knew nothing about it, and directly afterwards said, "Master had me up for this once before."
Prisoner's Defence. I paid my master on the Saturday night; he was by the butter-place at the time; he put the money into his pocket.
JOHN MARTIN re-examined. I am quite sure he did not pay me—I was in the parlour.
GUILTY. Aged 13.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury. — Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18470816-1950]
1950. JOHANNA SULLIVAN was indicted for stealing 1 half-crown and 1 sixpence; the monies of Edward Oliver, from his person; having been before convicted of felony.
EDWARD OLIVER. I live in Gurney-street, Poplar. I went into the Arrow public-house, at Poplar, with some friends, at half-past two o'clock in the morning, and saw the prisoner—I did not go in with her—she asked me to have a glass of gin, I did so—se went out together, down Blackboy-lane—while we were talking together, she put her hand into my left-hand pocket, and took out sixpence and a half-crown—I am sure they were there five minutes before—I said, "You have taken my money out of my pocket"—she said, "By G—I have not"—I said, "By G—you have"—a constable came up, and I gave her in charge—I had nothing to do with her more than I have now—my waistcoat was buttoned.
JAMES WILLIAM ANDREWS ( policeman K 104.) About half-past two o'clock in the morning I was in Blackboy-lane—Oliver gave the prisoner in charge, for robbing him of a half-crown and a sixpence—she said she had not got a farthing in her possession, and I could search her—I felt the money inside her gown—I said I should not search her; I should take her to the station, and a woman should not search her—she took a pin out, and if I had not taken the money from her bosom she would have dropped it—I took out half-a-crown, a sixpence, and fivepence halfpenny in copper—I asked her how she accounted for it, she made no answer—she was in liquor, Oliver was sober—she made no charge against him then, she did next day, before the Magistrate—going up to the office next morning, she told me she was in liquor, and did not know what she was about.
Prisoner's Defence. He said he would give me half-a-crown to take him home; he then said he could not go, and asked me to stop in the street; I said I would not, and he said he would knock my eye out if I did not give him the half-crown back.
WILLIAM WATKINS ( policeman.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction—( read — Convicted Sep., 1844, and confined nine months)—I was present at the trail—she is the person.
GUILTY. ** Aged 23.— Transported for Seven Years.
