NEW COURT.— Tuesday, August 24 th, 1847.
PRESENT —Mr. Alderman LUCAS; Mr. Alderman FAREBROTHER; Sir CHAPMAN MARSHALL, Knt., Alderman; Mr. Alderman GIBBS; Mr. Alderman WOOD; and Mr. COMMON SERJEANT.
Sixth Jury, before Mr. Common Serjeant.
[trialAccount t18470816-1952]
1952. ELIZABETH WILLIAMS was indicted for stealing 31 artificial flowers, value 10 s.; 1 skirt, 4 s.; 50 yards of ribbon, 1 l.; 3 pairs of stockings, 3 s.; 2 handkerchiefs, 1 s. 6 d.; 1 pair of bracelets, 2 s.; and 1 pair of gloves, 1 s.; the goods of James Stephenson, her master.
MR. WILDE conducted the Prosecution.
JAMES STEPHENSON. I am a linendraper, and live in Arbour-terrace, Commercial-road—the prisoner was in my service about nine months—she had been at home a few days previous to the 9th of Aug. with erysipelas in her foot, and her sister Jane attended to the children—on the 9th of Aug. I went out, and detected Jane committing a robbery—I then went with an officer to the prisoner's mother's house, and found these flowers, and other things—I saw the prisoner with her hand under the bed, between the bed and the sacking—I said, "Let me look there?"—she had previously said there was nothing there belonging to me—I there found some other things—she said her mother brought the flowers from Kingsland.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Do you identify these flowers? A. I can identify the boxes that they are in by their having my mark on them, and the flowers I believe to belong to me—they are good French flowers—Scott was in my service as well as the prisoner, and had the same opportunity of robbing me that the prisoner had—the prisoner's mother was never in my employ—I have found that Scott has been robbing me—I found the things at the prisoner's mother's house, 19, Vincent-street—Scott did not live there.
JEREMIAH CLANCEY ( policeman K 153.) I went the prisoner's mother's, and found these things in these boxes—the prisoner said they were only a lot of rubbish she had got from Kingsland.
GUILTY. Aged 18.— Confined Nine Months.
[trialAccount t18470816-1953]
1953. MARIA SCOTT and JANE WILLIAMS were indicted for stealing 9 1/2 yards of cotton, value 2 s.; and 1 pair of boots, 3 s.; the goods of James Stephenson, their master.
MR. WILDE conducted the Prosecution.
JAMES STEPHENSON. The two prisoners were in my service—in conesquence of the illness of Elizabeth Williams, Jane Williams came to assist with the children—on the 9th of Aug. I heard the children cry, in consequence of Jane upsetting the chaise—I went out and told her to take the children back, and I saw she had a parcel under her arm—I said, "What have you got there?"—she said, "Nothing"—I took it from her, and found these boots and this print—she had laid the parcel in the chaise—the articles are quite new, and had been taken from my shop.
JANE WILLIAMS — GUILTY. Aged 14.— Confined Three Months.
SCOTT — NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18470816-1954]
1954. MARIA SCOTT was again indicted for stealing 18 pairs of gloves, value 15 s.; 1 shawl, 8 s.; 1 parasol, 2 s. and 2 bonnet-falls, 1 s. 6 d.; the goods of James Stephenson, her master.
MR. WILDE conducted the Prosecution.
JAMES STEPHENSON. The prisoner was in my employ—in consequence of the other robberies, I was induced to search her box—I found all these articles( produced).
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. The two servants slept in the same room? A. Yes—the box in which these things were found was open—there were two beds in that room—I found some of these things marked with William's initials—the prisoner has been in my employ three years, and was always honest—I will take her again.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18470816-1955]
1955. GEORGE JOHN NAYLOR was indicted for embezzling 40 l. 10 s.; the monies of Anthony William Clark, his master.—2nd COUNT, for stealing 1 10 l. bank note, 11 sovereigns, and 1 half-sovereign; the monies of Anthony William Clark, his master.
ANTHONY WILLIAM CLARK. I am a surgeon, and live in Cheapside—the prisoner was my assistant. On the 19th of July, a bill of 40 l. 7 s. 6 d. became due—the acceptor of the bill called on me that day, and I being out he wrote a note, which enclosed a check for 19 l. and a sovereign, and begged I would get the difference to meet the bill, naming a gentleman who would lend it me—he left the note on my table, informing the prisoner or what he had done—the prisoner went out afterwards to pay the bill—I never saw the check—there was no other person there who could have taken the check—I received the note about half-past four o'clock—the check was then gone—it has been changed—the prisoner told me he had got it, and I sent him out to take the bill up with it—I have never seen the check since—it was on the London and Westminster Bank—I do not know the names of the parties, it is a company—the prisoner had the 10 l. note and the ten sovereigns of Mr. Richards, who is present—he is the person who I was to borrow the money of—the prisoner told me he had the check, and Mr. Richards was to give him the money, and he was to go and take the bill up—he did not do so—the bill has been dishonoured and returned—I am the drawer of it—this produced is it.
Cross-examined by MR. METCALE. Q. Where did you find this letter? A. On my desk—it was not sealed—the prisoner told me he had got the check and the money—Mr. Richards had already given him the money, and asked me if he was right in so doing—I said, "Yes," and told the prisoner to go and take the bill up with it—the prisoner had authority to receive money for me—he was in the habit of doing so—if anybody called to pay him money he would have received it—he was authorized to receive it for such a purpose as this—I had implicit confidence in him.
JOHN THOMAS KING. I wrote this check for 19 l.—I enclosed it in a letter to Mr. Clark—I made the check payable to the prisoner, for the purpose of making a payment at the bank—it was enclosed in a note addressed to his master—it has been paid, and returned to me—I had requested Mr. Clark to let Naylor go and take this check—the prisoner being before me, it was quite immaterial to me who I wrote it for—it happened to be so.
COURT. Q. Though you wrote it for Mr. Naylor, who was it for? A. For Mr. Clark.
ROBERT RICHARDS, ESQ. I am a barrister—on the 19th of July I paid the prisoner a 10 l. note, ten sovereigns, and a half-sovereign—I should say I paid it to the credit of both the parties—Mr. Clark had not authorized me to pay any—I was aware of the purport of the letter which Mr. King had written—I went in about three o'clock, and found Mr. King writing the letter—I advised him to finish his letter, and let it go forward—I paid the money—I was sure that Mr. Clark would recognize it the next day—I was aware of the letter—I would have acted upon it.
COURT to MR. CLARK. Q. Whose check was this? A. I am the drawer of the bill—I presume the check was my own—the prisoner told me he had got the check—I told him to go and take up the bill—the 20 l. 10 s. I presume was also paid to me—I sent the prisoner with 40 l. 10 s. to take up the bill with the check, and 20 l. 10 s. which I had got of Mr. Richards—he told me he had paid it for me.
MR. METCALF. Q. What were the duties of the prisoner to you? A. To make up medicines and any drugs that might be made up for sale; to go out for me, in fact to do anything and everything I ordered him—if he had refused to take up this bill, or to do any other menial work for me, I should have compelled him.
COURT. Q. If he had not done this, should you have discharged him? A. Yes—he had done it for me often before.
MR. METCALF. Q. He was taken by you to assist in you professional duties? A. Yes—I should have discharged him if he had not done a duty of this sort, because I considered it was what he ought to have done for me—I should not have conceived he was bound to go and take up a bill—he might have said, "That is not physic"—but no master I think would retain his servant an hour if he had refused.
COURT. Q. Did you employ him as your servant, and did he take the bill as such? A. Yes.
MR. METCALF. Q. Did you employ him as a servant, or as in assistant in your medical profession? A. As my servant—I think had he in any way refused to do this, and I had dismissed him, I should have had to pay his month's salary—this was one of those sort of things that no medical man could have kept an assistant half an hour after, if he had not done it.
GUILTY of larceny. — Confined Six Months
[trialAccount t18470816-1956]
1956. MARTHA SEERS was indicted for stealing 8 cigars, value 9 d.; the goods of Rachel Dallen, her mistress.
WILLIAM DALLEN. I am a licensed victualler. I act for my mother Rachel Dallen, who lives at Chelsea—the prisoner was her servant—on the 12th of Aug., I missed some cigars from the cigar-box—I found them amongst the ginger-beer bottles, at nine o'clock at night—I afterwards gave the prisoner into custody.
Cross-examined by MR. O'BRIEN. Q. How long has the prisone lived with your mother, is it fifteen or sixteen years? A. I do not know—I believe my sister is here to give her a character—my sister is married, she has not been living under the same roof with me, and I should be sorry for her to do so.
GEORGE LINDON. I am pot-boy to Mrs. Dallen. On the 12th of Aug., the prisoner came to me, and said she ad got eight cigars concealed amongst the ginger-beer bottles, and if I would accept of them she would be much obliged to me—I told my master—she said she could not get out at the back door, as it was locked—she often goes through the stable.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you not take these cigars out in a tray? A. No. my master took them—the prisoner found some cigars, and gave them to me—I do not smoke.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18470816-1957]
1957. JOHN SAUNDERS and HENRY WILLIAMS were indicted for stealing 1 pair of shoes, value 5 s.; 1 pair of boots, 8 s.; 1 apron, 6 d.; 1 souvenir, 5 s.; 1 thimble, 1 s. 6 d.; 1 brooch, 6 d.; 1 chain and locket, 1 l.; 12 hooks and eyes, 6 d.; 1 purse, 2 d.; and 6 keys, 6 d.; the goods of Macnamara Fitzgibbon: and 1 coat, value 15 s.; 2 handkerchiefs, 6 d.; 1 pair of trowsers, 10 s.; 1 shirt, 5 s.; and 1 waistcoat, 5 s.; the goods of Alfred Haskell.
ALFRED HASKELL. I am barman at the Golden Horse, in Aldersgate-street. On Friday night, the 14th of Aug., the prisoners came to the house about twelve o'clock, they said they came from the Blackwall Railway, and asked for a bed—they went to bed, I went to bed about one o'clock—I heard them talking in a suppressed whisper, though I could not understand what they said—the next morning I was down in the bar, Mrs. Witcher came down, and said there was somebody in Mrs. Fitzgibbon's room, for she tried to open the door, and it was pushed against her—I went up and saw nobody, I then came down to my room, which is on the second-floor, and found my clothes where gone out of the box—I came down and fastened the front door—I said to some coal-heavers who were there, "Don't let anybody out, we have been robbed"—I let the officer know, then ran up, and saw Williams putting some things under the bed where the prisoners had slept—I went to the bed, and found a bunch of keys—I called Mrs. Fitzgibbon, and she identified them—I then found these trinkets under their bed—I had seen Williams putting them under the bed, Saunders was standing by him—my boots, coat, waistcoat, and other things, were found by Mrs. Fitzgibbon hidden in my bed—there was nobody who could have removed these things but the two prisoners.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. You know nothing about Fitzgibbon's things? A. No further than what Mrs. Fitzgibbon had said—I got up about five o'clock; I heard of the things being found in my room, about eight o'clock—there were other persons living in the house, and coming to the house—there was a lodger in another room; he is not here—there is a female servant in the house; she is not here—Mrs. Fitzgibbon is here.
COURT. Q. The coat, handkerchief, shirt, trowsers, and waistcoat are yours? A. Yes, and they were found in my room, and just down by the prisoners' bed was my waistcoat, and a silver thimble in it.
MR. PAYNE. Q. Do you mean to swear that you found your waistcoat in their room? A. Yes, just down by the side of the bed—it was a black cloth waistcoat—I am sure it was mine, it was not Mr. Fitzgibbon's—he does not live in the house at all—it was mine, and had been in my room—the rest of my things Mrs. Fitzgibbon found—I had never seen the prisoners before.
HARRIET FITZGIBBON. I am the wife of Macnamara Fitzgibbon, and am living with my father, Mr. Walker, at the Golden Horse—I went up to the prisoner's room, stood on the landing outside the door and received the bunch of keys from Haskell—I went into the next room—I found the boots and shoes, and Haskell's clothes in his bed—these trinkets and bunch of keys which were found in the prisoners' room are mine.
Cross-examined. Q. I thought you did not go into their room? A. I did not go in—I stood on the stairs—Haskell gave me the apron of trinkets,
and the keys, outside the room—I saw him bring them out—I consider the property is mine—it is not my father's.
SAUNDERS — GUILTY. Aged 28.
WILLIAMS — GUILTY. Aged 21.
Transported for Seven Years.
(There were five or six other cases against the prisoners, for robbing public-houses.)

MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
HENRY BLAKE PARSONS. I am clerk to H. and J. Johnson and Co., of Bush-lane—on the 13th of June, 1846, I received this check inclosed in this envelope and this letter—I know the prisoner's writing—this check for 50 l. and letter are his writing—in exchange for the check inclosed in this letter, I filled up this check of my employers'—it was signed by Mr. Butler, one of the partners of our firm—I gave it to the bearer of the letter from the prisoner—the prisoner had a letter of credit from the house of Nathaniel Johnson and Son, on our house—it was in consequence of that letter I gave this check.

MR. BALLANTINE stated that he should be unable to resist the evidence, and consented to a verdict of

[trialAccount t18470816-1959]
1959. CHARLES CAHILL was indicted for embezzlement.
MR. CHARNOCK conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN CRIDIFORD. I am a perfumer, and live at Bristol—I deal with Mr. Burton, of Greek-street, Soho—on the 19th of Jan. I paid the prisoner 2 l. 1 s. 3 d., on his account.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. How long had you known the prisoner as a traveler? A. About six months—he appeared an active diligent person, pursuing his master's interests as well as he could—he received this money a few minutes after nine o'clock at night—the debt I owed was 12 l. 1 s. 3 d.—I arranged that I would pay the 2 l. 1 s. 3 d., and send him a bill for 10 l. to Reading.
JAMES ALDER. I am a perfumer at Windsor—I know the prisoner, as traveller to Mr. Burton—on the 21st of Jan. I paid him 4 l. 15 s. 8 d., on Mr. Burton's account.
Cross-examined. Q. Was there any discount allowed to you? A. Yes, 5 per cent.—it was paid in the evening.
HENRY BURTON. I am a perfumer and live in Greek-street—the prisoner was my traveller—it was part of his duty to receive money, and account for it on his return—Mr. Cridiford and Mr. Alder were my customers—the prisoner never accounted to me for 2 l. 1 s. 3 d. received from Mr. Cridiford—he brought me a bill of 10 l.—there is nobody but me to whom he could have accounted—there was 4 l. 15 s. 8 d. due from Mr. Alder to me—the prisoner paid me 4 l. 10 s. of that.
Cross-examined. Q. Is it usual to allow 10 per cent. on perfumery? A. He had a discretionary power to sell, at 5 per cent. or 10 per cent., or to sell at net cash, but all he received he was to account to me for—he was not
in the habit of postponing the accounting till he was about to set out on other journeys.
Q. Look at this paper ( producing one), and then tell me whether you did not occasionally delay to settle his accounts for some time? A. This refers to the accounts he had out with him on his journey—I could not furnish him with every item when he went, but I sent part of them after him—when he came back, it was his duty to account to me for everything he had received—I never delayed to settle with him from the return of one journey till the commencement of another—his salary was 1 l. a week, and a commission of 5 per cent. on all sales which he effected above 60 l. a-week, and 15 s. a-day for travelling expenses—I should have been very happy to have accepted those terms myself—I did not tell him that the 5 per cent. on the sum above 60 l. per week would produce him 2 l. or 3 l. a-week—I told him, if he exerted himself, he might get a very respectable living—when he found he could not collect 60 l. a week, I altered his salary to 30 s. a-week, and dropped the commission altogether—I believe he began in 1844—I cannot undertake to say that in one week he did not do business to the amount of 100 l., but we should take the average of the journey—the weeks were to be averaged while his salary remained 1 l. a-week—I wrote to him in June, about his making some bad debts, and he wrote me word that he would give me notice to quit my employ—after that he wished to stay again, and urged it very much on me; I did not urge it on him—I had been out of town, and on my return I looked into the books and found the confidence I had placed in him had been betrayed—I would not see him—I referred him to my solicitor, and sent Manley my foreman to him, to go into the accounts—I should not have refused to receive the amounts that were alleged to be due, I could not afford to lose them—the prisoner did not come to me in pursuance of an appointment I made with him—his brother called on me—I am not aware that I desired the prisoner to come to me the next day to settle these deficiencies—I have found out the deficiencies I have indicted him for since then, through going a journey—I am afraid I have much more to find out—I have been very lax with him, and have allowed errors which I ought not to have done.
NOT GUILTY.
(There were two other indictments against the prisoner, upon which no evidence was offered.)
[trialAccount t18470816-1960]
1960. HENRY COMSON was indicted for stealing 2 earthenware pots, value 1 s.; 1 earthenware pot lid, 3 d.; and 1 cup handle, 1/4 d.; the property of the London Dock Company.
MR. BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
MICHAEL GALVIN ( policeman H 110.) I am acting sergeant at the London Docks—a patrol of our force is stationed in the docks. On the 31st of July, about ten minutes before twelve o'clock at night, I was going my rounds, I saw a crate standing under a shed, and the prisoner near it—he had no business there—when I approached he left the crate hastily, and came towards me—I asked what he had been doing there—he said, "Nothing"—I said, "Let us come back and see"—we went, and I found a hole in the crate, the straw pulled on one side and disturbed, and down where he had stood were these two butter-pots—I accused him of stealing some—he said he was not stealing, but merely looking to see what the crate contained—I told him it was not on his beat, and if it were he had nothing to do with the
contents of it—I told him I should take him before the inspector, suspecting he had been stealing—he said, "Yes, I have," put his hand into his pocket and pulled out the top of one butter-pot, and begged for God Almighty's sake I would say nothing about it.
Cross-examined by MR. EVANS. Q. When you first saw him, where was he standing? A. By the crate—these butter-pots were close by the crate—the prisoner has been employed there twenty-one or twenty-two months—he is married and has a family—I do not know anything against him—I took him before the inspector—there was a further search made in his cupboard, and more things were found—he said he had found the whole on his beat, and he was looking into the crate to see what it contained.
WILLIAM SMITH. I am in the service of the Dock Company. I had seen the crate of crockery safe on Saturday afternoon the 31st of July, from two to four o'clock—I cannot say whether there was any hole in it—I did not examine it—I assisted in placing the crate under the shed—I did not notice any hole in it, or any straw protruding from it.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 26.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury. — Confined Four Months
[trialAccount t18470816-1961]
1961. ELIJAH KEATES was indicted for stealing 12 ounces of sugar, value 3 d.; the goods of the London Dock Company.
MR. BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
SAMUEL EGERTON ( policeman H 193.) On the 1st of Aug. I was on duty in the London Docks—the prisoner was on duty as a constable there—he had been doing duty on the East-quay the night before—he came to the station—I followed him, and saw him go to his cupboard, unlock it, put his hand in, pull something out, put it into his hat, and put his hat on a shelf over the dresser in the kitchen—he then said he would go and buy something for his breakfast—he put on his hat and was going out—we met the inspector in the passage, I told him—he ordered the prisoner to pull his hat off, and in it was this sugar—the inspector asked him where he got it—he said he picked it up the other night as he was coming off duty on the North-quay.
Cross-examined by MR. PARNELL. Q. What time was this? A. Between seven and eight o'clock on Sunday morning—I was about a yard and a half behind him—I should say the cupboard is about a yard wide—he opened the door quite wide—I saw that plainly—there are six samples of sugar—he was in the same division as I am, and was engaged at the same place—his pay as a policeman as 18 s. 7 1/2 d. a week—he had been seven or eight months employed in the docks.
GEORGE MADDOX. I am an inspector of police. I saw the prisoner's hat taken off, these samples were in it; he said he did not know what it was—I found it was sugar—he said he picked it up in the docks—it was his duty to take care of the property in the docks, and to report anything he found to me.
Cross-examined. Q. How are these things got at? A. These samples are nailed on the outside of each cask, with a nail through them, and are samples of what the sugar is in the cask—if he picked any of these up, it was his duty to bring them to me.
DANIEL RUDKINS. I am an officer of the Dock-Company. I know some sugars were landed partly damp, and a sample was drawn and nailed on the
cask till the sampling was completed—these would remain on the top till the lotting was completed.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 28.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury. — Confined Three Months
[trialAccount t18470816-1962]
1962. ALICE M'LEAN was indicted for stealing 1 watch, value 8 l.; the goods of Frederick Leopold Slater, from his person; and WILLIAM M'LEAN as an accessory after the fact; Alice M' Lean having been before convicted of felony.
FREDERICK LEOPOLD SLATER. On the 25th of July, at half-past eleven o'clock, I was coming down the Commercial-road—Alice M' Lean and another female spoke to me, we went to a public-house and drank—we came out, they still stopped, and kept talking to me—the other woman then left—I felt for my watch, and it was gone—I said to Alice M' Lean, "You have been taking my watch"—she said, "No, it must be the old woman"—I said I would give her in charge—she begged and prayed, and went down on her knees, and said she would give me the watch—she took me down to Bluegate-fields, and said to an old woman there, "Will you go and see if Mr. So-and-So is there"—she went, came back, and said he was not—Alice M' Lean then ran to a door-way—I ran, caught her by the shoulder, just in the door-way, pulled her into the street, and called "Police!"—William M' Lean then came out, struck me, and knocked me down—I am sure the prisoners are the persons.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. There was another woman with Alice M' Lean? A. Yes—she was away when I missed my watch, and Alice M' Lean said she would try and find the old woman—I lost my watch in Sutton-street, not more than half a mile from where William M'Lean knocked me down—he said, "What are you doing with this woman?" and said he was a policeman.
WILLIAM CHARLES POTTER ( policeman K 212.) I went to 4, Bluegate-fields, and found the two prisoners, and another man in bed—I said to Slater, "Which is it"—he said, "This is the man, and this is the woman"—the woman was undressed, the man had his coat off—I said they must go to the station, the woman for robbing Slater, and the man for striking him—the man said he never struck a man in his life—the woman was going to put on a bonnet and shawl that were there—Slater said, "It is no use your putting on that bonnet and shawl; this is the bonnet and shawl you had on before."
JAMES JOHN BLAKE ( policeman K 137.) I produce a certificate of Alice M' Lean's former conviction—( read—Convicted on the 8th of May, 1843; and confined one year)—she is the person.
ALICE M'LEAN — GUILTY. Aged 21.— Transported for Seven years.
WILLIAM M'LEAN — NOT GUILTY.

1964. JOHN EASTWICK was indicted for stealing 1 peck of oats, value 1 s.; the goods of Thomas Guiver, his master; and WILLIAM SHEARMAN , for feloniously receiving the same, knowing them to be stolen.
THOMAS GUIVER. I am a coach-proprietor, at Cheshunt. I keep my horses at the Ship-inn, Tottenham—Eastwick was my horse-keeper there—I thought it necessary to put some marked papers amongst my oats—I have some oats here which have the papers amongst them.
Cross-examined by MR. PARNWELL. Q. Does Eastwick feed his horses at Tottenham? A. Yes—I bring up the oats, and he cuts his own chaff—he had been about five months in my service.
RICHARD SINCLAIR ( policeman N 321.) I saw Mr. Guiver on the 14th of Aug., and I gave him some pieces of paper—on the morning of the 17th of Aug. I saw Shearman about the street—I watched, and saw him stop at the Ship, at Tottenham, about two o'clock in the morning—he remained there till about four o'clock—soon after he left I stooped his cart, and found these oats and this chaff—the papers were in the oats—I went and took Eastwick, on a charge of robbing his matter—he said he had not, he had only given Old Billy some sweepings—there were five pieces of paper found in the oats—I saw one piece picked out.
Cross-examined by MR. PARNELL. Q. Is the Ship a regular coaching-house? A. Only Mr. Guiver's horses feed there—other horses stop there—there is one ostler employed by the publican.
Cross-examined by MR. METCALFE. Q. Did you understand who he meant by Old Billy? A. I know Shearman goes by that name—I have known him some years—he said he was seventy-eight years old—I never heard anything particular against him—I had him in custody once about a macintosh—he was in company with a woman that had it—he was discharged, and examined as a witness.
(Eastwick received a good character.)
EASTWICK — GUILTY. — Confined Six months.
SHEARMAN — GUILTY. — Recommended to mercy by the Jury. — Confined Three Months.

halfpence—the beer came to 3 d.—one of those sixpences was marked—Saunders went and brought back three penny-pieces—two sixpences were gone.

. ROBERT WRIGHT ( policeman B 99.) A female sent me to the prosecutor's—he gave the prisoners in charge—he told me that they had robbed him—I searched Groves; while I was doing so, Saunders gave up the money to Mr. Parham.
JAMES PARHAM re-examined. Saunders gave up one of the marked sixpences to me, and one other, which he said belonged to me.
Cross-examined. Q. When you accused him, he gave you the money directly? A. Not directly; I said it was no use denying it—one prisoner was on one side of the counter, the other on the other—it was at super-time.

1968. JOHN STRIDE RIPLEY was indicted for stealing 2 handkerchiefs, 2 s.; 2 pairs of gloves, 1 s. 6 d.; 1 oz. of pins, 3 d.; and 1 brooch, value 1 s.; the goods of Robert Eames Gear, his master.
ROBERT EAMES GEAR. I am a linendraper, and live in Seymour-place, Camden-town. The prisoner was my apprentice—I received 40 l. with him—he came to me about four months ago—in consequence of something, I sent for a policeman, and gave him into custody—his box was produced an opened—two pairs of silk gloves, two handkerchiefs, and a brooch were found in it; which were my property—he had not the least right to take them—he took hold of my arm, and said, "Forgive me for this offence; it has been the first time, my father will pay for all.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. His box was unlocked? A. Yes; and was in a place where there were other boxes; a place that every-body in my employ went to—it was no room; it was a lobby—the policeman asked the prisoner if he had any objection to have his box searched—he made no reply then; he afterward said, "No"—he fetched it after the policeman had asked him two or three times for it—I have three persons in my employ—one has served me as an apprentice, and is with me for another three years—I have three beside the prisoner—I advertised for an apprentice, and his father applied to me in consequence—I have had the 40 l.—his father made a remark about paying half of it—I wished to have it all, and got it.
Q. I believe his father called on you a short time before this? A. Not a short time—I did not give him a good account of the prisoner—I said he was of a bad temper, and that that was the only fault that was in him.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. At the time the father called on you, had you any reason to suspect the boy's honesty? A. No—I had no desire to get rid of him, and found no fault—what gave me a suspicion was his taking glove-boxes out of my shop.
MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Did you see him take them? A. No, but he acknowledged it—here are two letters, which are in his writing—they were left in the front room over the shop, one directed to me, and one to my wife, on the 20th of June, or thereabouts—they do not refer to anything in this indictment.
SAMUEL GILLMAN ( policeman S 76.) I was called in, and the prisoner was given into my custody—he brought in his box himself, from a place adjoining the room where we were standing—these are the articles that were found, two pairs of gloves, two handkerchiefs, and a brooch—and a paper of pins was found on his person at the station—when he came to the gloves, Mr. Gear said, "Where did you get these?"—he said he got these on Monday last, and this other pair he said his mother gave him—Mr. Gear found his own mark on them—when he came to the brooch, he said it was his master's property—as he was going out of the room to put his hat on, he took hold of his master's arm, and said, "Forgive me, my father will pay for all."
Cross-examined. Q. When you proposed to look at his box, he said he
had no objection, and he brought the box in his own hands to his master? A. Exactly so—he opened it, it appeared to be unlocked—he said he got these gloves on the Monday—I do not recollect saying that before—I will not swear whether I said before that he said the other pair was given him by his mother, or he said the brooch was his master's or that he said, "Forgive me, my father will pay for all."
MR. PRENDERGAST to MR. GEAR. Q. Is this a brooch you wore? A. No—I should sell it in my shop for a shilling—there are many houses which sell these brooches—I have only two like this, and they are not the same pattern and color—I do not know whether this is stone or glass—these are the gloves—they were just in this way—this pair appear to have been worn—I saw them in the prisoner's hat on Sunday, with my mark on them—I did not speak to him about them then—I sent for the policeman on Thursday morning—these white gloves are new.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Do you believe them to be your property? A. Yes, they have a mark on them—I have no doubt the whole are mine.
NOT GUILTY.

1971. ANN SULLIVAN was indicted for breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Mary Hammond, at Westham, and stealing therein 2 half-crowns; and 1 apron, value 5 s. 6 d.; her property; having been before convicted of felony.
MARY HAMMOND. I live in the Almshouses, at Westham—on the 20th of Julty I lost an apron and two half-crowns—the apron has not been found, it was on my table, and the half-crowns in a box in the table drawer.
ANN ESSEX. On the 20th of July I was looking out of window, and saw Mrs. Hammond go out—the prisoner was standing at the corner of the turning—she then went and tried Mrs. Hammond's door, then opened the window—a gentleman was standing there—when he went away she got in at the window—I went and saw her inside—she came out, and I asked her what she was doing inside—she said she was not inside—I said I had seen her—she called me a d—d liar, and went away.
HANNAH HARRINGTON. On the 20th of July, at five or six o'clock in the morning, I was putting something out—I saw the prisoner in the privy, fast asleep—I went down again afterwards, and she was walking backwards and
forwards at the back of the Almshouses—she then came and seated herself on the tombstone for some time—when I went out she sat with her back towards the gate and her hands behind her.
THOMAS FORRESTER ( policeman K 229.) I took the prisoner, and charged her with this robbery—she said she had not committed it; that she did not go in at the window.
Prisoner's Defence. I was an out-patient of the London Hospital; I was going home, and this policeman followed and took me in charge.
CHARLES COLLIER ( policeman K 30.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction—( read—Convicted 15th June, 1846, and confined three months)—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY. Aged 47.— Confined One Year.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18470816-1972]
1972. SARAH GRANGER was indicted for unlawfully attempting to drown herself.
GEORGE FREEBODY. I live at Stratford, and work for Mr. Tanner—on the 26th of July, about six o'clock in the evening, I saw the prisoner struggling with another woman—she got away from her, and jumped into the river Lea—I saw her go under water, jumped in, and took her out—I had never seen her before—I am sure she is the person.
JURY. Q. How deep was it? A. About four feet six or eight inches—she jumped from a place as high as I am, and went right under.
MARY BEACHLEY. On the 26th of July, in the afternoon, I was with the prisoner at the Green Man—her husband came up, and I heard words pass between them directly—she upbraided him with forbidding her to see her child—they had many words, but I did not hear all—she was in a very excited state—she had been drinking previous to coming to Stratford—her husband and her walked on towards the river, which was in his way to his mother's, where his business took him—they parted, and she said she would drown herself—I reasoned with her as long as I could, and held her as long as I was able, but she got away from me, and jumped into the water—I do not believe she was conscious of right or wrong at the time, from the state of excitement she was in.
Prisoner. You were in liquor yourself; I accidentally fell in; I rolled out of your arms and fell in. Witness. I have stated the truth—I was sent for, not to drink, but to try to prevail on the mother to allow the prisoner to see her child—she promised me if she could see her child for a few minutes she would return home peaceably—she is separated both from her husband and child—the child is four years old.
GEORGE WIGGENS ( policeman.) About half-past six on this evening I went to the river and found a man had just taken the prisoner out of the water—she was very much excited, and had been drinking a little, but not so much as not to know what she was about—she was insensible for a minute or two after she was taken out—I took her to the house, her husband came to the door—I asked for admittance, he slung to the door, and said she should not come in—he came out, struck me, and told me I might take her where I liked—I got another constable, and we took her to the station in a truck.
Prisoner's Defence. I came from London to see my little girl, and to take her out for a couple of hours; they would not let me have her; I was drinking at a public-house with the witness; we afterwards went away, but returned to the beer-shop again; I was very much in liquor, and did not know that I saw
my husband; I was told I was not sensible for a long time after I was taken out.
GUILTY Aged 23.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury.— Judgment respited.

[trialAccount t18470816-1973]
1973. ELIZA HUTCHINSON was indicted for stealing 1 purse, value 6 d., 6 sovereigns, 1 half sovereign, and 2 sixpences; the goods of Thomas Aspling, having been before convicted of felony.
THOMAS ASPLING. I am seaman, and live at North Shields. On the 1st of July, about ten o'clock at night, I was at a public-house at Deptford, drinking with two other men—I saw the prisoner and some other women there—I do not know what public-house it was—I was sober, but had been drinking—I left the public-house with the prisoner and another girl, and the two men, and they went with me to get a lodging—I walked along by myself—we went to a house in Dock-street, Deptford—we got there about eleven o'clock—we all went in together—I had some beer there—I was sober after drinking that—I had six sovereigns, a half-sovereign, and two sixpences, in a purse in my jacket pocket—I slept in that house alone—the prisoner was in the room when I went to bed, nobody else—I laid my jacket on two chairs in the room—she was in the room then—I went to sleep—I was awoke about half an hour afterwards by the mistress of the house, she told me something, in consequence of which I felt in my jacket pocket, and missed my purse and money—I am quite sure it was in my jacket when I put it on the chair—this now produced is the purse which the money was in, it is mine—the prisoner was not in the room when I awoke—a witness showed me the purse—it had no money in it then.
Prisoner. Q. Did not you say you could not pay me till morning? A. No—I was not drunk—I did not drink any rum—we did not have six pots of ale and half-and-half—I did not give you the money to mind till morning, you took it from my jacket pocket—I did not count it out in my hand when I was going to put it into my pocket, and say, "You may as well take it as me"—I drank nothing but beer.
WILLIAM THOMAS ARTHUR ( policeman R 202). On the 2nd of July, about one o'clock in the morning, I was on duty at Deptford, and saw some one running round the orchard as I was coming up King-street—I ran round to the other end of the orchard and met the prisoner—I knew her, and called her by name—she directly turned round as though some one had been assaulting her, made use of bad language, and said, "Now, Mr. Shelly, come and strike me again; I have assistance now; here is the police"—I judged from what she said, that somebody was after her, but saw nobody—I asked her the reason, and she told me it was Shelly and his woman—I said, "You shall not be assaulted, or anything of the sort"—I could see no one, and asked where they were—she said at a public-house where they had been drinking—as soon as I got into Dock-street, Deptford, the woman that keeps the house called after me to know if I had anybody in custody—I had the prisoner with me at the time—she could hear what the woman said—I said, "This is quite a different thing altogether"—I took her to the prosecutor, he directly said that was the girl; that he had been
robbed of nearly 7 l. by her—the landlady said that being a stranger to the house, and finding she had gone out and left the man, she thought something was wrong—the prisoner said she had not robbed the man—I took hold of her left hand, which she had got very tightly clenched, and felt there was coin of some sort in it—she resisted very much indeed, and I was obliged to get other parties to assist me—I found a sovereign, a half sovereign and two sixpences in her hand—she said it had been given to her by Batty Conder—there is such a man—she did not tell me he was the person who had given her the money to take care of for him—I asked her where the purse was—she strongly denied any knowledge of having the purse.
Prisoner. I was not three yards from the house; you asked me what was the matter; I said a man of the name of Shelly and his woman had been blowing me up for what I was not guilty of: you said, "Come along with me, you shall not he insulted;" did not you? Witness. Yes—it was right round at the back part of the orchard—you were without your bonnet—you did not come towards the house till you were taken—you were not drunk.
SARAH EVERLY. I am the wife of John Everly, and live at the station-house, in the Greenwich-road—between one and two o'clock in the morning in question, I was desired to search the prisoner—she was very reluctant to be searched, and said she had nothing whatever about her—I found a purse in her bosom—she said it was her own, that she kept it to keep duplicates in, and said there were two in it then, but it was quite empty—it was shown to the prosecutor.
Prisoner. Q. Did not I say, if I had got a purse it was the one I kept my tickets in; and when you found it, I said, "That is my own?" A. No, you said the duplicate were there.
Prisoner's Defence. The man gave me the money to mind till the morning, and I took it, as I was drunk and he was drunk; he said, "We wen't go to bed till we have had a drop more rum, bring half a pint;" he said, "Get it in a half-pint bottle for 1 s.;" I went to the first public-house, and could not get anything to drink; that was when I stood and saw the policeman; I was coming towards the house again; I did not attempt to run away; I no friends.
WILLIAM FREDERICK SEELEY. I was formerly in the police—I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction—( read — Convicted April, 1845, of larceny from the person, and confined one year)—I was present at the trial—the prisoner is the person.
GUILTY. Aged 22.— Judgment Respited.

1976. ANN HARRISS was indicted for stealing 1 5 l. Bank-note, the monies of Thomas Whitewood, her master.
MARTHA WHITEWOOD. I am the wife of Thomas Whitewood, of South-street, Greenwich—the prisoner was in my service. On the 9th of Aug., a gentleman called to pay me some rent—he paid me a 5 l. note and 17 l. in silver—I put it all into a pocket-book, and placed it in a sideboard-drawer, in the parlour—the prisoner knew of it—I said before her that I had received it—she had access to the room—the drawer was left open—next day I placed the pocket-book under lock and key, without examining it—I do not know whether the note was in it then or not—next day, Wednesday the 11th, I went to the pocket-book, and missed the 5 l. note and 3 s.—nobody but the prisoner and my daughter knew anything about it—a constable was sent for, and the prisoner said, in my presence, she had not seen it—on Friday, the 13th, she came into the kichen, and asked me if I would forgive her if she paid me back the whole of the money—I asked her if she took the money—she said, "No"—I said, "Can I receive of you what you say you have never taken of me? No, I cannot do it"—in about a minute she came again, begged me to forgive her, and said she had taken the note and 3 s.—I had told her that day, that if she had been tempted to take it, if she would replaced it, I would forgive her—I said nothing about her confessing, only about replacing the money—I sent for a policeman in the evening, and gave her in charge for something else—she is an orphan—I had no character with her—mine is the first place she has been at.
GEORGE GOODE. I took the prisoner on Friday evening at Mr. Whitewood's—I told her she was charged with stealing a 5 l. note—I cautioned her that I should tell the Magistrate what she said—she was anxious to tell the truth, and said she had taken the note and burnt it, and had taken other things besides.
Prisoner. I am guilty. GUILTY

MARTHA WHITEWOOD. [trialAccount t18470816-1978]
1978. WILLIAM JENKINS was indicted for unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin.
MESSRS. BODKIN and DAWSON conducted the Prosecution.
HANNAH DROUET. My husband is a licensed victualler, and lives in Hon-lane, Woolwich—on the 3rd of June, between nine and ten o'clock at night, the prisoner came and asked for a small glass of spruce—it came to 1 d.—he
gave me a shilling—I gave him the change—he went away—I put the shilling into the till—I am quite sure there was no other shilling there—I looked particularly at it, and said I did not think it was good—the prisoner said yes, it was a good one—I sounded it—Gladwin the policeman came in and asked me to allow him to look at the shilling—I gave it him—when I took it out of the, I still found it was the only shilling there—I marked it and gave it the officer—this is it.
WILLIAM GLADWIN ( policeman R 122.) I saw the prisoner in Mrs. Drouet's house—she gave me this shilling.
JAMES WATSON. I keep the While Horse, at Charlton—on the 4th of June, about nine o'clock in the evening, the prisoner came to my house and asked for a pennyworth of spruce—I served him—he tendered me a piece of money purporting to be a shilling—I gave him a 6 d. and 5 d. in coppers, in change—he left the house—my ostler stood behind me and said, "I have a suspicion that this is a passer of bad money, he has been here before"—I never put the shilling out of my hand—I sent my son and the ostler after the prisoner for a mile, and I followed leisurely after them as far as a toll-gate—in a quarter of an hour I saw the prisoner again—he had avoided the main road—I took him coming through the gate—I said, "Young man, you have been to my house and passed a bad shilling for a pennyworth of spruce"—he denied being at my house at all, and did not know me—I took him back to my house—on the road he asked me to let him look at the shilling—I said, "No, I have got the shilling, you must give a better account of yourself"—he asked me to give it him back, and said he would give me a good one.
HENRY WADLOW ( policeman R 267.) On the 4th of June I apprehended the prisoner at Mr. Watson's—I produce this bad shilling, which I received from Mr. Watson—I found on the prisoner a shilling, a sixpence, and fivepence in halfpence, all good—he said he had not been in the town more than two hours.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. You questioned him about having been at Mr. Watson's, and he said afterwards he had been there? A. Yes—this case has been standing over for two months.
CALEB EDWARD POWELL. I am inspector of coin to the Royal Mint. These shillings are both counterfeit, and have been cast in one mould.
GUILTY. Aged 17.— Confined Four Months.
[trialAccount t18470816-1979]
1979. JOHN WILLIAMS was indicted for embezzlement.
ROBERT WILLIAMS. I am cashier in the service of Thomas Wilkinson Kershaw and another. They live at Greenwich; the prisoner was in their service—on the 5th of July I made a communication to my master, and in consequence of that six penny pieces, and twelve halfpence were marked—about four or five o'clock that evening I saw the prisoner serving a customer—he called for change for a shilling, and farthings—I took him five pence, one halfpenny, two farthings, and a sixpence—I did not see what he took from the customer—I think it was three farthings—he put it into the till, and went to another customer and sold her a yard of calico for 4 1/2 d.—he called for change for sixpence—I saw him take 4 1/2 d. of the customer—I watched him—he only put a portion of it into the till—I could not see how much, but I saw part of it in his hand—he went to another part of the counter, put up some hose, put his hand behind him, and dropped some of the coppers from his hand into his coat pocket—I think it was more than one piece—an officer was sent for.
Cross-examined by MR. ROBINSON. Q. Have you had any conversation with the customer? A. No—the customer is not here—when the prisoner dropped the money into the till I was at my desk, which is higher, and about a yard and a half from the till—I am not always there, but there is always some one there, except when they go to take change—I was about three yards distant from the prisoner when I saw him drop something into his pocket.
ANDREW NESBIT. I am in the prosecutor's employ—on the 5th of July I marked twelve halfpence and six penny pieces—the prisoner asked for change—the cashier left the desk and gave it him—I requested that he should come away from the till—I noticed the prisoner go to the till and drop something into it—he then went to another part of the shop, put his hand behind his back to his back to his coat-tail-pocket, and dropped some money into it—I communicated what I had seen to Mr. Lowe—the prisoner was taken up stairs—Mr. Lowe requested him to put the money out that he had in his pocket—he did so, and there was 1 s. 4 1/2 d. in coppers—amongst it was one penny, and, I think two halfpence marked—Mr. Lowe accused him of robbing him—he said he had not—I pointed out the marked money to him—he made no reply—he had no business with that marked money in his pocket—he could not by right have put any money he received into his pocket.
Cross-examined. Q. How near were you to the till? A. Within about two yards, and I was about the same distance from the prisoner when he dropped the money into his pocket—to the best of my knowledge that was the same pocket from which he took the money afterwards.
JURY to ROBERT WILLIAMS. Q. Was the prisoner in the habit of bringing you the money? A. No—if he wanted any change he called for it—we do not check under sixpence—if he takes under sixpence he puts it into the copper till—if a customer comes and the prisoner calls for change, I take him the full change—he took 4 1/2 d. from the customer, he should have put it into the copper till—I saw him hold some of the money after the customer was gone—if he took 7 1/2 d. from a customer he would give me the sixpence.
GUILTY. Aged 35.— Confined Six Months.

1982. ROBERT BROWN was indicted for stealing 1 purse, value 1 d.; 3 sovereigns, 2 half-sovereigns, and 2 half-crowns; the property of George Moffat.
GEORGE MOFFATT. I am a gunner in the Royal Artillery. On the 13th of July I went to bathe at Plumstead, I laid my clothes on the ground, near a tree—I had in my pocket a purse in which was three sovereigns, two half-sovereigns, and two half-crowns—when I got out of the water my purse and money were gone, my pockets were empty—my attention was called to a small piece of paper lying beside my clothes—this is it.
THOMAS KNIGHT. I am a sergeant in the Artillery. I went with Moffatt
that day—the prisoner was there with a second party, he did not bathe—he went with me and a comrade to London—he had plenty of money, he spent about 9 s.—I saw this piece of paper picked up by Moffatt—it is part of an answer to a latter—( read —"I received your letter, and you wanted to know the reason your wife did not answer your letters; I went to her, and she said she—")
MARY DOUDE. I am servant at the Duchess of Wellington. On the 14th of July, between six and seven o'clock in the evening, the prisoner came out of a room at our house, and gave me two sovereigns, and 14 s. 6 d.—he told me to take care of it.
FERRAND BROOK GIRLING. On Wednesday evening, the 14th of July, a person came to me and bought this ring for 13 s.—I believe it was the prisoner.
Prisoner. He said before the Magistrate he did not allow me.
THOMAS GRIEVE. I am a private in the Artillery—I have written letters to the prisoner from his wife—I know this piece of a letter—I wrote it to the prisoner—I was at the bathing place that day—the prisoner wanted to leave the ranks—the bombardier would not let him go, he asked two or three times—we marched up to the front of the barracks, and stopped a quarter-of-an-hour or twenty minutes and a non-commissioned officer came and told the corporal we were to be detained—we were marched into the rear—the prisoner saw his comrades getting searched, and put something into his nouth—he said it was tobacco, but I can swear it was not—he left the ranks without being searched, and went out of the back-yard.
MICHAEL GARVEY ( policeman R 87.) I took the prisoner—I found on him this gold ring, one penny, one half-penny, a snuff-box, two purses, and an envelope box.
The prisoner put in a written defence, declaring his innocence, and stating that part of the money was given him by his cousin, and part he had earned in his trade as a shoemaker; and that, being in liquor, he left it at the public-house for safety; that he afterwards took part of it out, and bought a ring and purse with it.
GUILTY. Aged 20.— Transported for Seven Years.

[trialAccount t18470816-1984]
1984. JOHN CROUT, WILLIAM HARMAN , and RICHARD ADAMS were indicted for unlawfully having in their possession c. rtain naval stores, the property of our Lady the Queen,
MESSRS. GODSON and PETERSDORFF conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM KENDALL. I am a private in the Royal Marines, stationed at Woolwich Dockyard—on Friday evening, the 30th of July, about half-past seven o'clock, I saw the prisoners in a small boat, alongside the pinnace, in the river—they were shifting things from the boat to the pinnace, and from the pinnace to a barge, it was canvas, rope, and metal of some description—the men were first in one boat and then in another—I gave information to Ellis, and he and I watched them—I saw one of the prisoner with a shovel—he appeared to be digging a hole in the ashes, and putting rope and canvas in the hole—they were all three in the barge at the time—I was on the
wharf at the dockyard, about a hundred yards from them—my attention was directed to a copper-pot in the after part of the small boat that I first saw them in—I afterwards saw it in the barge, thrown on the top of the ashes—the barge was placed under my care at three o'clock in the morning—I then found in it an oar, a pot, a block, some rope and canvas, and half a barrel of pitch on the ashes.
Cross-examined by MR. ROBINSON. Q. You saw this copper-pot first in the boat? A. Yes—I did not see it in the barge for eight or none hours after—I do not know whether other persons had been in the barge—there was a policeman in charge of it—I was relieved at nine o'clock, and another sentinel placed there—I had stood and looked at what was going on—I saw everything distinctly—I saw them all three in their turn shifting the canvas and rope—I saw that from where I was standing—it is my duty to watch—I saw a bread bag removed, and what appeared to be metal.
STEPHEN ELLIS ( policeman R 162.) I am employed at Woolwich Dock-yard—on Friday evening, the 30th of July, about half-past seven o'clock, Kendall gave me information, and my attention was directed to a boat alongside of a barge—there were three persons in it; Court was one, I knew him—I cannot speak positively to the others—I saw them take something from the boat and put it on board the barge—I suspected it was not right—the three persons then left the barge, got into the boat, and rowed it to the Bell Water-gate pier—I ran round, and saw Court in the boat, which belonged to him; it had his name on it—the other two prisoners left the boat, and got on the pier, and Court was handing them some things which appeared to be sailors' clothing—I asked them what they had got, they said property they had brought from the Spiteful steamer—I asked them their names—they gave the same names that they did afterwards to the Magistrate—I am quite sure the prisoners are the persons.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you take them into custody then? A. No, they were taken the next day—i had never seen Adams before, to my knowledge—he gave the name of Henry Adams, and when he came before the Magistrate, he gave the name of Richard Adams—I am sure he is the same man.
WILLIAM JAMES BUDD ( police-inspector.) I was on duty at Woolwich Dockyard, on the 30th of July—some information was given me, and I called on Sergeant Glascock and Turner to go on board a barge—I went on board about eight o'clock, and found a great many ashes, some rope, canvas, copper sheathing, and a piece of copper bolt, in a corner of the barge; and a great many clews and lashing in the force part of the barge—they could not well cover them, there were so many—these produced are the articles—there was this pot and this pipe, and this sheet-copper—I then left the barge in possession of the constable—this is the canvas and rope.
Cross-examined. Q. Is this the greater portion of the canvas? A. I think about half of it—there was something alongside the barge, which is used for putting ashes into, previous to bringing them to the barge—the barge was at anchor about one hundred yards from the shore—there was nothing between that and the wharf.
JOHN EDWARD GLASCOCK ( police-sergeant R 25.) I was on duty at Woolwich Dockyard, on Monday morning, the 30th of July, about eight or nine o'clock—I searched the barge which kendall pointed out—I had seen it several times previous—I first saw it about eight o'clock on Friday night, the 30th of July—Turner was left on board of it—I searched amongst the ashes, and had them all turned over—I found ninety clews and half a
barrel of pitch, a bread-bag, an oar, an eleven inch block, and a number of things, some of which have the Government mark—here is the broad arrow on the bread-bag, here is the worsted in the rope, but it cannot be seen before you open it, you can see nothing outside—I found a great deal of iron, some of it is marked, this sheathing is all marked, this block has the broad arrow on it.
DAVID ROY. I am a clerk in the Store-keeper's office at Woolwich—I have examined this property produced—this piece of copper is her Majesty's property, it is marked with the broad arrow, and this copper-pot also—this block is marked very distinctly—this sheathing has the broad arrow on it, the Government mark—this oar is marked, and this lashing is marked with the worsted thread—you can see it outside this.
JURY. Q. Are these marks generally known to every man on the river? A. Yes, very well known—this is all her Majesty's property—whoever is accustomed to get a living on the water, would know these marks very well.
Cross-examined. Q. The prisoners were employed by the contractor to remove things from steamers? A. Yes—ashes, and rubbish of every kind—Mr. Warren Hawker has been the contractor about eight or nine months—these things are stamped—I identify them from the particular form of mark—if you were to put an arrow mark on that brief, I should say it was the Government mark—the prisoners are only common labourers—it is not the duty of the contractor to take anything but ashes and dust—I am not aware that many things found in the dust and ashes, have been returned—it is arranged that if any little things are found they are to be returned—I am not aware of any being returned.
COURT Q. They do not include these oars amongst dust and ashes? A. No.
COURT — GUILTY. Aged 43.
HARMAN — GUILTY. Aged 34.
Confined Two Months , and fined 5 s.
ADAMS — GUILTY. Aged 18.— Confined One Month , and fined 5 s.
[trialAccount t18470816-1985]
1985. JOHN DANIELS, THOMAS MOON, JOHN JACKSON WILLIAMS, GEORGE ANGOVE , and PATRICK CALLAGHAN , were indicted for unlawfully having in their possession certain naval stores, the goods of our Lady the Queen.
MESSRS. GODSON and PETERSDORF conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM TURNER ( policeman R 56.) I was on duty at Woolwich Dockyard on Friday evening, the 30th of July, at the Watergate—my attention was drawn to the prisoner Daniels, who was on the upper deck of the ship called the Nymph, which was lying in the stream opposite the dockyard—she is a hulk, for the accommodation of seamen while ships are fitting out—the Spiteful steamboat was lying alongside the Nymph —the men on board the Spiteful had been paid off the some day, and there were none on board—I saw Daniels passing some rope and canvas down the accommodation-ladder of the Nymph into a boat, which was between the two vessels, moored at the bottom of the gangway, alongside of the Nymph —I saw one man in the boat, but I cannot say who it was, he appeared to be assisting in putting the things into the boat—I then procured the assistance of Sergeant Glascock, and went to pursue the prisoners in a boat—I had noticed Daniels get into the boat, and about ten minutes before the boat left, I noticed that there were four other men in it—when I got near to the boat I saw the prisoner Daniels in it, the four rowers, a female, and a man who is boatswain of the Spiteful steam-ship—they
landed the two passengers at the Bell Water-gate—there had been no opportunity for anybody to have got into the boat from the time I noticed Daniels handing the things down—whoever was in the boat must have been in it at the time I noticed it originally—I followed the boat with Glascock—it first went to Bell Water-gate, Woolwich, where the female and the boatswain landed—I then called to Daniels, I did not know his name—I called to the persons in the boat, and told them to stop and return, that I might see what property they had got in the boat—I got no answer—they did not return—they went in the direction of Barking Creek—we did not follow them—I saw them go about a quarter of a mile—we got within about twenty yards of them—I was near enough to identify the persons—I saw Daniels, I could not recognise any other person—I saw a quantity of rope and canvas in the boat—it is impossible for me to tell whether it was the same that I saw handed down the companion-ladder—it had the same appearance—they had not an opportunity of getting it out of the boat again—it must have been the same—I did not lose sight of them—I made a statement to an officer at Woolwich, I received orders, and went on the Saturday morning to the Royal George revenue-cutter, at Barking Creek, in company with Sergeant Glascock—I searched the cutter, but did not find any rope or canvas—I saw all the prisoners on board the cutter, but whether they were the men who were in the boat, with the exception of Daniels, I cannot say—I left Glascock on board.
JOHN EDWARD GLASCOCK ( police-sergeant R 25.) I received information, on Friday night, the 30th of July, from Turner—I took a boat and rowed round the Nymph —a boat had just rowed away from it, with the five prisoners, and another man and a woman in it—I was near enough to it to identify all the prisoners—I have seen them about, and know them all by sight as belonging to the coast-guard; and Daniels had come to the yard several times—they rowed down the middle of the stream, towards Barking Creek—I approached to within three or four yards of their boat—I hallooed to them, and Angove stood up—I told him to stop, and asked him what they had got in the boat—he said some old stores, which they had been up to Limehouse to get—I saw some rope, similar to that produced, in the stern-sheets of the boat—I did not notice anything else—after I had called to Angove, and he had said that, he sat down, and they pulled away as hard as they could—the next morning I went to the Royal George, at Barking Creek—I obtained leave of the officer in command, and searched her with inspector White—I pulled up a flap and saw a board loose, I pulled it away and saw this canvas—the men were then mustered on board—the prisoners were amongst them, with the exception of Callaghan—the rope and canvas were brought on deck—Williams said he took it for the use of the service, and he did not think there was any harm in taking it—Lieutenant Simpson was the officer in command.
COURT. Q. Did you ascertain, in presence of the four prisoners, that the officer on board knew or did not know of these things being there? A. No; he did not know—he rendered me every assistance—he told me he had drawn no stores at all.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. You knew all the prisoners perfectly well? A. Yes; and I dare say they knew me—I have not the least doubt that they would have stopped the boat, only they were a little the worse for liquor—the best part of these things were found in the hold of the vessel.
RICHARD WHITE ( police-inspector.) I went on board the Royal George, with Glascock—I assisted in searching, this rope and canvas was found in the hold of the vessel—the hold is covered with a deck—I had the men mustered in the after-cabin—the prisoners were all present but Callaghan—these stores were on the deck—I asked Daniels what account he had to give of the rope and canvas which were found in the hold—he stated that on Friday evening he had been on board the hulk Nymph, that he had been on the deck and walked about twenty minutes, that he neither saw anything put into the boat out of the bulk, or taken out of it, at Barking Creek—I next asked Moon what he had to say—he said he went on board the Nymph to see his brother-in-law, who was boatswain; that he was there twenty minutes, and he did not see anything put into the boat; but when he came into the boat he saw the things in it, and he had lent a hand to put them into the cutter at Barking Creek—Williams and Angove said they were on board the Nymph, and that the boatswain told them they could take as much rope as they wanted, to make swabs; and they took it, and thought there was no harm—swabs are to swab the deck with—the boatswain's name is William Kemmish.
DARIUS PENNIAL ( Thames policeman, 25.) I was present on the morning when the Royal George was searched—I searched round about the shore, and found a piece of canvas in a reed-bed, about thirty yards from the vessel's side.
WILLIAM KEMMISH. I was officer on board the Nymph, on the 30th of July—I saw Daniels and the other prisoners on board, in the evening—I did not give them leave to take the cordage and canvas from on board.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you not know that they took it? A. I did not—I was in the cabin, and Moon, my brother-in law, was with me—I should say he knew nothing of these being taken, he was in the cabin the whole time with me—I was not on deck at all—I did not see the boat go off—I cannot say where this rope and canvas was taken from—it was not under my charge—I did not see anything of it—I did not know it was on board—I neither looked into the boat when it came nor when it went away—I took no notice of the men going away—I did not come on deck when they went away—my brother-in-law bid me good night, and went away—my wife was with me I have found out since that she went with these men and landlord at the Water-gate—I did not see her off—there was only myself and a boy on board—I told the boy to go and land my wife—I should say these are old stores—I cannot say that they have been dismantled from the Spiteful —I cannot say whether the hold of a vessel is the place where these things are kept.
COURT. Q. Then you were in charge of the vessel, five men came on board of it, and stepped off with your wife, and you made no inquiry about it from beginning to end? A. No.
MR. GODSON. Q. How near was the Spiteful to the Nymph? A. Close along side—it had come from foreign service—when a vessel comes from foreign service there are directions to take the old stores to the dockyard.
COURT. Q. What was done with the contents of the Spiteful when she was dismissed? A. The stores were returned, I believe—the ship was not dismantled, nor the masts and yards—the warrant-officer was in charge of it.
MR. GODSON. Q. What medal is that you bear? A. For service in China—I have been twenty-eight years in the service.
LIEUTENANT SIMPSON. I am officer of the Royal George cutter —on the
morning of the 31st of July some stores were found on board—they did not belong to the Royal George.
Cross-examined. Q. How long had the prisoners been in the service? A. Daniels fifteen years; be always bore a most excellent character—Williams has been eleven years; and the others some years—I have had no complaints against them—it is not usual in the service to exchange things from vessel to vessel—we have received Queen's stores for our vessel, but these we did not receive—these were in the hold, where we always put our stores—there are five hatchways open to it—we have a great quantity of stores; I suppose 200 l. worth—this canvas is fit for nothing but to make swabs—if I had seen it lying in the hold as it is, without hearing any account of it, I should have desired my men to work it up for swabs—we had no swabs on board; they were very much wanted—we received our stores two days after this—we get them once in twelve months—I do not exactly know the value of these things—I suppose they would have fetched 15 s. or 16 s.
MR. PARNELL. Q. What were the wages of these men? A. guines a-week—I believe Daniels has been in the service nearly forty years—there has been no other charge against him while he has been with me, nor before, that I have heard of—if the prisoners had got these stores and wanted to appropriate them to their own use, I think they might have been concealed where no one could have found them.
MR. GODSON. Q. Is not this piece of canvas nearly new? A. I think it is—it would not be used for swabs—I do not know what it would be used for—it seems to be something of hammock-cloth-( the witness here opened it)—it is a cover made to cover a sail—it is nearly new—I suppose it is worth 6 d. or 8 d. per yard—we do not have our stores from the storehouse, but from private contract—never from the naval stores,
DAVID ROY. I am a clerk in the storekeeper's-office, at Woolwich—I have examined this bundle of rope—it is marked with the worsted thread, the usual mark in the navy—it is worth about 20 s.—this canvas is nearly new—it has the Government mark on it, a blue streak of paint in a seperntine form, that is the usual mark in the navy—its value to us would be 10 s.—neither the boatswain nor any person at the hulk have authority to pass things from the naval stores to the coast-guard—the coast-guard have their stores by private contact—we have nothing to do with the revenue coast-guard, they are under the Custom-house—they do not have their stores from the naval store.
Cross-examined. Q. In the ordinary course, would not this be passed to the dockyard, as old stores from the Spiteful? A. Yes, and he sold shortly afterwards; but not in this form, it would be cut up—this canvas has been used as awning, or something of that kind—it would be cut up in pieces about two yards long.
COURT. Q. What is the use of cutting it up? A. If it were unserviceable, it would be cut up—it is no use to the dockyard as it is—it has been made, I suppose, for their own convenience.
(Mr. George Wright, Deputy-governor of Newgate, gave all the prisoners, except Moon, good characters.)
DANIELS — GUILTY.
MOON — GUILTY.
WILLIAMS — GUILTY.
ANGOVE — GUILTY.
CALLAGHAN — GUILTY.
To enter into recognizances to appear to receive judgment when called upon.
[trialAccount t18470816-1986]
1986. LEONARD PROVO was indicted for stealing 18lbs. weight of mustard, value 6 s.; and 2 casks, 1 s.; the property of John Green.
WILLIAM SWAISLAND. I am in the service of Mr. John Green, an oil and colourman, at Woolwich. On the 5th of Aug., the prisoner came to the shop—he wanted two casks of mustard, and two gallons of mixed pickles—I gave him them from under the counter—he afterwards said he wanted three bill-heads, which I gave him from off the file—he took one, and wrote on it Mr. Vine's name, what was under it I do not know—he said I was to go with him and receive the money—when I got to the Ordnance Arms, he took the mustard, and told me to go on with the pickles to Mr. Vine, at the Dover Castle—When I got there, the pickles were not ordered.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE Q. Had you known the prisoner previously? A. No—my master is not here—I have been shop-boy there four years—this happened twelve months ago—I do not know whether the prisoner had had dealings there before—I only lost the mustard—I am sure the prisoner is the man—the mustard was in casks, it was worth 2 s.—I was not to bring back 8 s. and the bottles—I did not hear that—I said I was going with the things—I let the prisoner have the mustard—my master was not at home—the prisoner said he came from the other house to get these things.
COURT. Q. Did you let him have them, because you were to go with him to get the money? A. Yes.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Had you full authority at this shop to sell articles? A. Yes—I had not to ask my master's permission—if there is nobody in the shop I am allowed to sell things—I am not allowed to trust people.
JAMES VINE. I keep the Dover Castle public-house, at Woolwich. I did not order the prisoner to bring any mustard or casks—Swaisland brought two gallons of pickles—they were not ordered.
Cross-examined. Q. Did your wife order any mustard? A. No.—she was present when the pickles were brought, and the prisoner was left at the Ordnance Arms—I returned the pickles back.
WILLIAM GLADWIN ( policeman, R 122.) I have been searching for the prisoner, and have not been able to find him for nearly twelve months.
NOT GUILTY.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18470816-1987]
1987. WILLIAM ASHWORTH was indicted for embezzlement.
CHARLES STEEL. I am a baker, at Greenwich—the prisoner was in my service—he left me on the 19th of June—he had authority to receive money on my account, and to give an account of it to me every night when he booked his bread—he did not give me 3 s. 4 d., as received from Mr. Dalton, on the 17th of June, nor 3 s. 4 d. on the 18th of June, from Mrs. Field—Mr. Dalton pays for his bread every day—I called out the name of Dalton, in booking the bread on the 17th of June, and the prisoner said, "Four loaves"—I asked whether they were paid for—he said, "No"—I asked him again on the 18th, in the same manner—he had to pay 3 s. 4 d. on the 17th, and 3 s. 4 d. on the 18th—I did not say anything to him about Mrs. Field, but he has never accounted to me for what he received from her,
Cross-examined by MR. METCALF. Q. What time did the prisoner enter your service? A. The second time, in the middle of May—I paid him his wages every Saturday night, and he had a loaf a day—I discharged him on
Saturday morning, the 19th of June, for getting intoxicated and spoiling my bread—his wages were not paid on that Saturday, and they have not been paid—they would have been on that night—it was 19 s.—he has not asked for it.
JOHN DALTON. I am a grocer, and live in the Old Woolwich-road. I dealt with the prosecutor for bread—the prisoner used to come to me daily with it—I paid him 3 s. 4 d. on the 17th of June, and 3 s. 4 d. on the 18th of June, for Mr. Steel's bread.
FRANCES FIELD. I am the wife of Alfred Field, of East Greenwich—I dealt with the prosecutor—the prisoner used to bring the bread—he came and received a payment, but I do not know on what day—it was due on the 12th of June, and I paid it on the following week.
Cross-examined. Q. You do not know whether it was the beginning or the latter end? A. No.
GUILTY. Aged 31.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury. — confined Three Months
