[trialAccount t18470816-1990]
1990. ANN BLACKMORE was indicted for feloniously killing and slaying a certain male child; also charged on the Coroner's Inquisition with the like offence.
MR. COOPER conducted the prosecution.
JOHN ROBINSON LEADAM. I am a surgeon, in Tooley-street, Southwark, In July last I was called to see a child of the prisoner's, it was just dead—I examined it—I understood that it had been strangled—there were some crease-marks about the neck, in the situation of the cap-strings, which appeared to have been tied rather tightly, but not sufficient to cause death—I made a post-mortem examination, next day—I discovered nothing particular externally—on removing the scalp I found extravasation of blood on the right side of the head—I immediately examined the scalp, and then observed two or three small bruises on it, very slight, not sufficient to be observed before—I then examined the skull, and found it fractured to the extent of about two inches on the right side—in my opinion, that was sufficient to cause death, it might be caused by a blow, or fall on any hard substance.
prisoner. I am subject to fits. Witness. I do not know her all—I saw her when I was sent for—she was then deeply intoxicated, so as not to know very well what she was doing—she was able to walk—she seemed hardly
conscious of what she was about—she said she did not know the child was dead.
MARY DONOVAN. I am a window, and live in Bull-court—I know the prisoner. On the 6th of July, about twenty-five minutes past ten at night, I saw her in the yard—she had taken a drop, but was not too far gone then—she was able to walk—she had the child in her arms, holding it round, close to her—I took it out of her arms, and said, "Take care of your child, and yourself too"—she did not seem satisfied to part with it, and I gave it her back again, settled it to her in her arms, and closed the shawl up—she was not holding it in a dangerous way, but the shawl was hanging down, and I settled it about the baby—she then went towards the Bull public-house, and I lost sight of her—I saw her again in about five minutes, going by the thoroughfare that there was—there is a drain there, she missed her footing, and fell down with the baby in her arms—the baby happened to come on its head; it fell under her—I went towards her, she picked herself up immediately, and went through the thoroughfare—I heard the child groan after she rose—I did not see her again till the Tuesday morning following.
COURT Q. Was she behaving kindly towards the child at the time you settled it to her, as you say? A. Yes—what I did to the child was out of kindness, and she took it as such—she took the child kindly to her bosom, then walked away, and fell by accident—a sober person might have had just such a fall in the same place, there is no light there—it is an open drain, which a sober person might have fallen over.
NOT GUILTY.
[trialAccount t18470816-1991]
1991. JAMES BRUCE was indicted for feloniously killing and slaying Mary Allsworth; he was also charged on the Coroner's Inquisition with the like manslaughter.
MR. PLATT conducted the prosecution.
ANN DIGWEED. I am the wife of William Digweed, a grocer, of High-street, Lambeth. On Saturday night, the 10th of July, about half-past ten, I was behind the counter—the prisoner came in very tipsy, and staggering—he asked how I did, then turned round from me to the boy Blake, who was sitting on a sugar-tub by the door, and began pulling him about, roughing up his hair on each side, and saying, "You are the chap that serves out the coffee"—he pulled him off the tub, and pushed him against the street-door, he then dragged him back to the counter, then took him round the head with his arm, and the boy being shorter than himself, he had that power over him that he reeled him round and round, and I thought both would have fallen into the street—he took the boy off his feet—they both went out into the street together, and as they went out, they met an old lady, who was stepping on the edge of the path with her supper-beer in her hand, and with one blow they went against her, and knocked her backwards—they were both together at the time the blow was struck—the prisoner had the boy round as he went out of the shop, and went against the old lady—it is a small shop, and the counter is near the door—I could see pretty plainly, there was a lamp alight at a public-house opposite—the old lady turned out to be Mrs. Allsworth, who I knew very well as living four doors off—I saw her fall, with her jug in her hand, as if she had fallen from some high place, she fell direct on the back of her head—I ran out to her, and heard her say, "Oh my head; this will be my death-blow"—I lifted her up, and she was taken home—the prisoner
went away as far as William-street, which is one door from us, he was afterwards brought back by the boy—Blake said to him, "Come back, and see what you have done; you have knocked the old woman down"—his reply was, "What am I to do? am I to pay for 1/2 oz. of coffee? if I have hurt you, mother, God bless you!" and away he went—he was quite drunk—thinking she was not much hurt, I let him go—I went home with the deceased, and saw her bonnet taken off, it was very much dented in at the back—there was no blood—she was still groaning, "Oh, my head"—her daughter laid her on the bed—she died next morning.
Cross-examined by Mr. PAYNE. Q. Did you know the prisoner before? A. No, I had never seen him before—when he was roughing up Blake's hair he said, "A little whiting and blue would make you a good colour"—he seemed very foolish, and full of frolic and fun—the prisoner is much taller than Blake—he took hold of him and took him off his feet—when he knocked his head against the door, Blake said, "Now, then," and that was every word he spoke—he appeared to be quite in the prisoner's power when he was whirling round—they appeared to go round and round as if shifted by some machine—when he came back, and said what I have stated, he was mumbling about the boy, and appeared to be saying something about his getting the best of him—I did not hear him say to Blake, "Oh, it is you that rolled me out of the shop?"—Blake was not intoxicated, he was sitting as quiet as a child—the deceased had a bottle in one hand and a jug in the other, I cannot say whether she was sober—the street had been dug up, to repair the water-pipe, a place had been left lower than the other part of the road, and the deceased appeared to fall in that place.
MR. PLATT. Q. The deceased was near that spot? A. Yes, she must have had her foot on the edge of the path, for she spilt some of the beer on the edge of the path, directly opposite—her feet appeared to go just down by the door, and her head into this place that was not repaired—I do not think she stepped from the spot after she was struck.
COURT. Q. You state that the prisoner was drunk, and that what he was doing was in the nature of fun? A. Yes, it would appear like it—what he was doing to the boy was like what a foolish drunken man would intend as fun—it did not appear like an assault or ill-usage, but more in the nature of play—the boy was unusually quiet, according to his character in general—he let him go on, I think, on account of the things in the window, for I called out, "Mind the boxes, do not go through the glass, "
WILLIAM BLAKE. I am twenty years old, and live with Mr. Digweed. I was in the shop on Saturday night, the 10th of July, when the prisoner came in—he first spoke to my mistress, then turned to me, pulled my hair, pulled me off the tub I was sitting on, and pulled me from the door to the counter, and from the counter to the door; he then let go my hair, put his left-hand round my neck, and pulled me outside the door, and when he got me outside, be put both hands on my hair, gave me a pull towards him, and a shove from him—we both went out together round about—he did this against my will—I could not help myself, but I took it in the light of a joke.
COURT. Q. You thought it was the folly of a drunken man? A. Yes, and let him have his humour as he did not hurt me much—I did not care about it, and never intended to complain of it.
Mr. Justice Erle being of opinion that the prisoner was not proved to be engaged in any unlawful act at the time the accident occurred, the Jury found him
NOT GUILTY.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18470816-1992]
1992. HENRY JOSEPH KILLERBY was indicted for feloniously sending to Elizabeth Challenor a certain letter, threatening to kill and murder Elizabeth Spriggs, the daughter of the said Elizabeth Challenor; and that he had been before convicted of felony.
MR. PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
ELIZABETH CHALLENOR. I am the wife of John Challenor, and live at No. 45, White-street, Borough. I have a daughter named Elizabeth Spriggs, by a former husband—she is thirteen years old—I know the prisoner—I had occasion to make a complaint of him some time ago, with reference to my daughter—he was imprisoned for a year—he came out of prison on the 26th of Feb., I think—I received the letter in question by post, on the 13th of Aug.—this now produced is it—I had received one previously, on the 20th of July, and one before that—I do not know whose writing they are, but I had many letters before which were proved to be in the prisoner's writing, and I took these to be the same writing—the letters that were the subject of the previous complaint were in the same handwriting.
EDWARD KENDALL ( police-sergeant, A 30.) I am acquainted with the prisoner's handwriting—I have seen him write on one occasion—I believe these letters of the 20th of July and the 12th of Aug. to be the prisoner's writing.
COURT. Q. What was it that you saw him write? A. The direction of an envelope and his signature—it was at his father's house, before I apprehended him on the first occasion—it was done for the purpose of ascertaining his handwriting—I did not prove his writing on the first occasion—there was another witness who had seen him write on more occasions, a foreman in an establishment where he worked—he is not here to-day—the Magistrate said it was not necessary, and that I was sufficient—I believe these letters to be in the handwriting of the prisoner.
Cross-examined by MR. BRIALY. Q. You have never seen him write but once? A. Never, that was merely a direction and his name—it was in Sept., 1845—I requested him to write it, I have got it here—that is all I know of his writing, but I have plenty of writing here which was aworn to as being his, on the former trial.
MR. PAYNE Q. Was it when you went to apprehend him in Sept., 1845, that you got him to write? A. No, some time before, when I was making inquiry to detect the person who had sent the letters—he wrote the direction, "Miss Elizabeth Spriggs" and his own name, in my presence—having seen him write that, I have not the shadow of a doubt of these letters being his—the character of the writing is particular—I paid particular attention to his writing—I went for that purpose.
ELIZABETH CHALLENOR ( re-examined.)—The prisoner was apprehended on this charge on the 15th, I think—I have had conversations with him about it—I never spoke to him on the subject of the letter of the 12th of Aug.
Cross-examined. Q. Have you known him for any length of time? A. I never knew him to be the son of Mr. Killerby, till I saw him a year and a half ago—I think he is about nineteen years of ago—I have lived neighbour with his parents for twenty-two years, but never knew him—I do not know of any acquaintance that he had with my daughter—he never spoke to her, not at any period—I have not been in the habit of having any communication
with him, and know nothing of his habits—I never spoke to him till I visited him in consequence of his writing a letter to me about three weeks ago—I thought I would reason with him—he has always been a very close character, apparently, but I know nothing of him—with one exception, I believe he was a quiet, inoffensive person—I have heard that he is fond of reading—we have been extremely annoyed by him for some length of time.
MR. PAYNE Q. You know that he was tried and convicted here in Feb. 1846, for sending a threatening letter to you, for Elizabeth Spriggs? A. I do—I believe my case did not come before the court on that occasion—it was some other case upon which he was convicted—he was not tried upon mine.
The letter was here read, as follows— "Mrs. Spriggs, No.45, White-street, Borough—London, Aug.12th, 1847.—Mrs. Spriggs, —As I have before told you, I am happy to inform you that I know the residence of your daughter Elizabeth, and I can safely promise you, that before another two months has passed away, that your daughter will be in her grave; for as true as there is a God in heaven, I do intend to murder her, and before that time has passed away too, be the consequences what they may. Yours truly, Henry J. Killerby."
DONALD MURRAY ( policeman, M 119.) I apprehended the prisoner, on Saturday night, the 14th, on a warrant—I read it to him—it stated that he was to be taken for sending a threatening letter to Elizabeth Spriggs—he said he was very sorry for it, but he had no meaning in it
Cross-examined. Q. Did you hold him out any inducement to say that? A. No.
MR. BRIARLY called
HARRIET ELIZABETH KILLERBY. I am the prisoner's sister—he will be nineteen in Dec.—he is a very quiet inoffensive boy—he is fond of writing and reading—he reads novels—if he had wished to injure Elizabeth he could have done so very well, for she has been in the habit of coming and sitting in our room with us, and playing with me of an evening, up to the time she went to school—she has not been in our house since my brother came home in Feb.—before he went to prison, she and her sister used to be always with me, and he used to come and sit down and read, and take no notice of any one—she has been about our place, but he never spoke to her—he used to read Lloyd's Miscellany, and those sort of books—he has two large volumes of it, full of such nonsense as this—I have looked into them many times—there are tales about secret conspirators, persons that have made a great noise in the world, love tales, and tales about carrying off young ladies into caverns, but he was quite inoffensive notwithstanding this—he never intended injuring any one.
MR. PAYNE Q. He was mischievous kind of fellow, was he not? A. I do not know what you mean—he sent poison in three letters to three different young ladies, and was convicted in one case.
MR. BRIARLY Q. Was it ascertained that it was poison that was sent? A. No, I do not know that it was—it was thrown in the fire—it was sent to a Miss Ward.
EDWARD KENDALL ( re-examined.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction—( read—Convicted 23rd Feb., 1846, for attempting to administer poison to Elizabeth Clouter, and confined one year)—I was present at the trial—the prisoner is the person who was so convicted.
GUILTY. Aged 19.— Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutrix. — Confined One Year
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.

1993. PATRICK O'BRIEN was indicted for stealing 1 pair of boots, value 3 s.; the goods of John Booth, from the person of Thomas Augustus Booth; having been before convicted of felony.
AMELIA BATES. I am nearly eleven years old—I live with my father, James Bates. I recollect going out to play at the Belvidere ruins—I saw John Booth and another little boy there, and the prisoner and a boy a little bigger than him—the prisoner and the other boy took little Booth into a privy, sat him on the seat, and took off his boots—the boy cried—the prisoner said, "If you do not hold your tongue I will take and hit you"—he took out a knife to cut off the child's fingers—I saw the prisoner go away, I caught him—I knew him by sight—the other punched me on the head and got away—I saw the little boy afterwards—he had no boots on.
ANN BOOTH. My husband's name is John—I am the mother of the boy—he went out on Wednesday, to play—he had his boots on—when I saw him in the afternoon he had not got them on—they were my husband's property.
JAMES M'INTOSH ( policeman, H 98.) I produce a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction—( read—Convicted Jan. 1847, and confined one month and whipped)—I was present at the trial—he is the boy.
GUILTY. Aged 13.— Confined Four Months.
Before Mr. Common Serjeant.

HENRY TOOLEY. I live with my father and mother. Between eleven and twelve o'clock I saw Iredale and Wright go up to the barrow and take a loaf out—Baylis waited against a fence—Iredale walked towards Clapham with the loaf—Wright came to Baylis, who stood by the fence.
GEORGE SEARLE ( policeman, V 48.) I was called by a constable to assist him—I followed Wright two miles, took him to the station, and found a quantity of bread crumbs in his pocket.
ALFRED SPICE ( policeman.) I received information, and followed the prisoners for two hours—I saw them in company before they went to the barrow—there was a cart between me and the barrow—I did not see them take the bread.
NOT GUILTY.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.

[trialAccount t18470816-1997]
1997. WILLIAM INGRAM was indicted for stealing 1 ass, price 1 l. 5 s.; the property of William Edwards.
WILLIAM EDWARDS. I deal in coals, and live at Clapham. On Friday, the 5th of Aug., I had a donkey turned out on Clapham-common—I saw it safe at nine o'clock next morning, and missed it about half an hour afterwards—about four o'clock the same afternoon, I saw it in Smithfield—it is worth from 25 s. to 30 s.
EDWARD LAURENCE. I live in Park-place, Walworth. On the 6th of Aug., about twelve or half-past, I was in the Blackfriars-road, and saw the prisoner riding a donkey, with a man walking by his side—they both spoke to me—the prisoner asked me to buy the donkey—I said I did not know—he offered it for 12 s.—I offered all the money I had got, which was 8 s.—he said he would not take it—he went about forty yards further, then ran back to me, and said I should have it—there was an old bridle with it—I said, "I had got nothing to hold it with"—he said, "I cannot give you this, because my father said I was to take it home again," but as last I had it, to lead the donkey home with—I paid him the money, and came away with it—I took the donkey home, and put it in a little barrow to see if it would draw, but it laid down and was no use to me, so I sent a man named Simpson with it to Smithfield that afternoon.
Prisoner. Q. Did we halloo after you, or did you ask us whether it was for sale? A. You hallooed after me.
JURY. Q. Who did you pay the money to? A. To the big one—the prisoner said it was his father's, and he was not to take less than 12 s. for it—the other was not likely to be his father, he was only seventeen or eighteen years old—I do not know whether 8 s. was below the value—I never bought one before.
WALTER CHARLES DODD. I live in Union-street, East-lane, Southwark, I met Laurence in the act of buying the donkey—he paid the prisoner 8 s. for it—after that I heard the prisoner say, "Shall we halve the money here"—the other said, "No, go into the public-house"—they went in, and I saw no more of them.
JOHN MENNIE ( policeman.) On the 7th of Aug., the prisoner was brought to the station by Laurence, charged with stealing a donkey from Mr. Edwards—he said a man named Blaxted told him he was going to sell a donkey for Mr. Edwards, he afterwards said that he took the donkey and sold it for 8 s., and that all the money he received was a fourpenny-piece, two penny pieces, and a halfpenny.
Prisoner's Defence. I was going after my master, Mr. Bluff's, donkey, and met the man with a donkey; he asked me to go with him to sell it; I went with him, we sold it; I knew nothing about it being stolen; he gave me a fourpenny-piece, and 2 1/2 d., I said it was Mr. Edwards' money, and heaved it on the common; I would not have it.
JURY to MR. EDWARDS. Q. Did you see the donkey in Smithfield the same day you saw it at Clapham-common? A. Yes—I had no wish to sell it, and did not authorize anybody to take it to Smithfield.
GUILTY. Confined Four Months.
[trialAccount t18470816-1998]
1998. MARGARET M'CARTHY was indicted for stealing 1 coat, value 5 s. 6 d.; the goods of John Cartwright.
EMMA WHITE. I am servant to Mr. Sculley, of York-place, Old Kent-road. On Wednesday, the 11th of Aug., I was taking down the shutters of my master's shop, and saw the prisoner and a young man on the opposite side of the way—the man took a coat from outside Mr. Cartwright's door, and put it in the prisoner's lap—they crossed over towards Kent-street—Mr. Cartwright's man came over to me—I told him what I had seen, and pointed out the prisoner—I am sure it was her.
THOMAS RICHARDSON. I am in the service of Mr. John Cartwright, a clothier of Buckingham-street, Dover-road. On the 11th of Aug., at eight o'clock in the morning, I had put the goods for show outside the shop, and in about ten minutes missed a coat from the end of the stall—I crossed the road, made inquiries, followed the prisoner, she came back in about two minutes, I gave her into custody, and took her back to the shop—I have not seen the coat since—I saw no man.
HENRY BROOKER ( policeman M 109.) On the 11th of Aug., about eight o'clock, I was on duty in the Old Kent-road—the prisoner was given into my charge—I told her what it was for, and took her back to the shop—she denied having any hand in it—no coat was found.
Prisoner's Defence. I was not near the shop: I was coming down Kent-street away from it, and the young man tapped me on the shoulder, and took me to the shop, and the young woman opposite said I had stolen the coat.
EMMA WHITE re-examined. The prisoner was not taken till ten minutes after the coat was lost.
NOT GUILTY.
Before Mr. Common Serjeant.

[trialAccount t18470816-2001]
2001. ELIZA TRAINER was indicted for unlawfully uttering counterfeit coin.
MESSRS. CLERK and PARNELL conducted the Prosecution.
GRACE HAYES. I am the wife of William Frederick Hayes, a tobacconist, of Lambeth. On the 1st of July, the prisoner came to the shop for a pennyworth of tobacco and a bottle of ginger beer, which came to 2 d.—she gave me a bad sixpence—I bit it, bent it up, put it on a shelf by itself, and said, "This is a bad one"—she said, "Oh, I am sorry for it," and went out of the shop—in two minutes after she had left, I took it down and marked it with a knife—I gave the sixpence to the officer between six and seven o'clock the same evening—this now produced is it—here is the mark I made on it.
EDWARD JENNINGS. The prisoner came to my shop on the 7th of July,
at half-past twelve o'clock, for a quarter of an ounce of tea—she offered me a sixpence in payment—I put it between my teeth—she said, "Is it a bad one?"—I told her it was a very bad one—I told her I should detain her while I sent for a policeman—she went away—after she was gone, I bit the sixpence in two, and threw it away.
JOHN BARRETT. I am a tobacconist, and live in James-place, Clapham. On the evening of the 8th of July, the prisoner came to my shop for a quarter of an ounce of common tobacco—it came to 3/4 d.—she gave me a counterfeit sixpence—I saw that it was bad, and sent up stairs for a police-constable who was in my house—I gave him the sixpence—I made a rough mark of a B on it—this is it.
CALEB EDWARD POWELL. I am inspector of coin to the Royal Mint—these sixpence are both counterfeit, and cast in one mould.
GUILTY. Aged 18— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18470816-2002]
2002. ELIZABETH SIMPSON and CHARLOTTE ROCKWELL were indicted for a like offence.
MESSRS. CLERK and PARNELL conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN ROLES. I keep the White Hart Tavern, at Battersea. On Friday, the 6th of Aug., the prisoners came in together, called for a pint of porter, and one of them put down a shilling—I drew the beer before I took it up—I then looked at it, and saw that it was bad—I put it between my teeth, bent it up nearly double, and chucked it upon the sill of the bar window—I told them it was bad, and asked where they got it from—they said in coming along by the steamboat—I asked them how many more they had got—they said they had no more—they had half a pint of beer, and paid for it with a penny-piece—they went out—I gave the shilling to my pot-boy, and sent him to go round the square, and meet the policeman at the end of the lane.
WILLIAM GREEN. My master gave me a bent shilling on the 6th of Aug.—I went after the prisoners—I did not see a policeman—I afterwards gave the shilling to Walters.
ROBERT WHARTON ( policeman V 168.) I produce this shilling, which I got from Green.
ELIZABETH MALTBY. I am the wife of John Maltby, who keeps the Nag's Head, in the Wandsworth-road. On Friday evening, the 6th of Aug., the prisoner came and asked for half a quartern of gin—Simpson gave me a shilling in payment—I thought it was bad, and I bent it nearly double between my thumb and finger—I handed it to a gentleman standing close to me—he gave it back to me—I did not lose sight of it—I put it on the counter—Rockwell took it up, and pretended to put it into some part of her clothing—she handed it to Simpson, and I saw something thrown into the street—I have not seen it since—my husband came in in the midst of the transaction.
JOHN MALTBY. I am the husband of Elizabeth Maltby—I was called by her to the bar on the 6th of Aug.—I saw the two prisoners there—I wanted Simpson to give me the shilling she offered for the gin—she would not, she threw it past my head, and said, "There, can you swear to a shilling that is thrown in the street?"—the constable took one prisoner, I took the other—in coming out, Rockwell dropped two shilling on the step of the door—I picked one up—the other she kicked with her foot—I gave the one I picked up to Bartholomew at the station.
JAMES FINDLAYSON. I saw a shilling, picked it up, and gave it to the policeman.
Simpson. Q. Were not we going along the road, twenty yards from the house? A. When I overtook you you were.
CHARLES BARTHOLOMEW ( policeman V 180.) I was called to Mr. Maltby's, and found the prisoners there—as they came down the steps, I saw a shilling fall from Rockwell; Mr. Maltby picked it up, and gave it to me; one shilling dropped at the same time down by my leg, it rolled down on the road—I called the boy to pick it up; he gave it to me, and I put one in each pocket—these are the two shillings.
Rockwell. Q. You were in the house having gin and water; you were not called in. A. I deny that: I was called in; Mr. Maltby can speak to that.
CALEB EDWARD POWELL. The shilling produced by Wharton is counterfeit—these other two are counterfeit, and they are all three from one mould.
Rockwell's Defence. I never dropped them at all.
SIMPSON.— GUILTY. Aged 21.— Confined Six Months.
ROCKWELL.— GUILTY. * Aged 22.— Confined One Year.
[trialAccount t18470816-2003]
2003. MARY ANN MORRISON and BENJAMIN MORRISH were indicted for a like offence.
MESSRS. CLERK and PARNELL conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM NICHOLLS. I am a labourer, and live at Norwood. On the 12th of Aug. I was in the Holly-bush beer-shop there—the two prisoners came up to the door—the man came in, and called for half a pint of ale—he put down a half-crown—the landlord refused to take it—the man paid for the ale with a good sixpence, and took up the half-crown again—he went out-side, and he and the women drank the ale together—I followed the prisoners to Mrs. Cherry's—after looking in at the window, the women went inside—the man stood outside till she was about to come out, and then he went in—when he came out they went on to near the Woodman, to a beer-shop and chandler's-shop, kept by Bacon—the woman went in there, and when she came out they came to Mr. Paddy's—the man went in there, and the woman remained outside—the man bought something there, and when he came out they went away together to Mr. Trundle's, at the Swan—I followed and saw them go in there—I came back, and met the constable—I went with him to the door of the Swan—I went in, and asked Mr. Trundle if his money was right or good—Morrish turned and said, "What do you mean?"—Mr. Trundle said, "I have one half-crown they gave me; I am rather near-sighted, will you look at it"—Morrish took it from his hand, and said, "What do you want?"—I said, "That is a right one, I dare say"—the policeman came in, and he caught Morrish's hand—he threw the half-crown outside the door—two shillings dropped from Morrish, which I took up and gave to the officer the prisoners were taken into the back room and searched by the officer—I spoke to Mrs. Cherry; and she gave me a shilling, which I took to the officer.
Morrison. I took the half-crown, and said, "If it is worth nothing, it shall be nothing?" Witness. You did not take it; the man took it.
Morrison. It was me that threw it; he was throwing his hands about, but it came from me, not from him; and the two shillings fell from me.
Benjamin Morrish. Q. If you saw the two shillings fall from me, why did you not state it in my hearing? A. I saw them drop from you.
SARAH CHERRY. I am niece of Mrs. Cherry, who keeps a chandler's-shop
at Westow-hill, Norwood. On the 12th of Aug. Mary Ann Morrison came for a halfpenny-worth of sugar-candy; she gave me a shilling, I gave her the change—she turned from the counter, and the male prisoner came in, and asked me what time the train started—I told him, and he said, "We shall be just in time"—I put the shilling which the woman gave me into the till, but not into the bowl with the other silver, because I thought it was a bad one—I was in the act of biting it when Nicholls came in, and I gave it him—this is the shilling.
ELIZABETH PADDY. I am the wife of Thomas Paddy, a baker—on the 12th of Aug. the male prisoner came into the shop, asked for a biscuit, and gave me shilling—I gave him a sixpence and 4 1/2 d. in coppers—I gave the shilling to my little girl to put in a box, where there was no money but two sixpences and one half-a-crown—I saw the box after the shilling was put into it—I saw that there was no other shilling in it—the male prisoner lelf the shop, and Nicholls came in—I bit the shilling and cut it, and gave it to Clark, the policeman.
WILLIAM TRUNDLE. I keep the White Swan beer-shop, at Westow-hill, Norwood—the prisoners came on the evening of the 12th of Aug.—the male prisoner asked for a pint of ale—I served him, and he gave me half-crown—Nicholls came in afterwards, and I showed him the half-crown—while I was showing it, the male prisoner snatched it out of my hand, and said it was a good one—I did not see what became of it—the officer came in, I gave the prisoners in charge.
RICHARD ROBERTS. I am a gardener—I was in at Mr. Trundle's when the prisoners were there—I saw the male prisoner cast his hand, and I heard something chink outside the door—I took a candle, and searched—I picked up a half-crown, and gave it to Threadwell.
Morrish. Q. How far from the door did you pick it up? A. About three yards.
STEPHEN THREADWELL ( policeman.) On Thursday night, the 12th of Aug., Nicholls came to me—I went to Mr. Trundle's—the prisoners were there—I saw Trundle return something towards Nicholls—the male prisoner caught at it—I caught his arm, and he tossed a half-crown towards the door—I heard something fall, but as the prisoners stood both together I have no idea from which it fell—I received from Nicholls two shillings, which he stated he had picked up at the counter—he did not tell me from which it fell—I produce them—Roberts gave me this half-crown—this is the shilling I received from Nicholls, which came from Miss Cherry.
Mary Ann Morrison. It was me that threw it.
JAMES CLARK ( policeman P 108.) I produce one shilling, which I got from Mrs. Paddy—I found on the male prisoner two sixpences and ten shillings, all good; and on the woman, six sixpences, a fourpenny piece, and 2 1/4 d. in coppers, all good.
CALES EDWARD POWELL. This half-crown is counterfeit—these four shillings are all counterfeit, and from the same mould.
MORRISON — GUILTY. Aged 18.— Confined Three Months.
† MORRISH — GUILTY. Aged 21.— Confined Nine Months.
[trialAccount t18470816-2004]
2004. THOMAS HATTON was indicted for stealing 2 tons of lead, value 18 s.; the property of William Hall, being fixed to a building.
MR. PARNELL conducted the Prosecution.
WILLIAM HALL. I lived at Bayswater—on the 3rd of July I let some premises at No. 33, John-street, Surrey, to the prisoner—there was two ranges of building, one a workshop, the other a stable—the prisoner represented himself as a master cowkeeper, and took the place to make a dairy—he said he should bring in fifty cows—there was some lead in the gutters on the roof, and some round the skylights, some lead cisterns, and a lead force-pump—there was from three to four tons of lead—the prisoner was to have the place till the 10th of Aug. on sufferance before he commanded rent, and then to have it for three years—this was all put in writing—I went to the premises about ten days afterwards, and found the lead had been stripped away from the gutters and the cistern, and the lead pipe taken away off the pump—I did not find any cows there—the prisoner never brought a shilling's worth of property onto the place—there were no signs of preparation for a dairy—the almost that was brought in was about four penny worth of lime—I went to inquire where he had lived before, and I went to the premises a second time—I then found that even the paving-stones were being pulled up—I should say there were from two to three tons of lead gone—it was worth from 30 l. to 40 l.—I should say the stones were worth 4 l. or 5 l., but I understand he sold them for 33 s.—there was a man employed to put zinc on the gutters.
Cross-examined by MR. PAREY. Q. who was the tenant before the prisoner? A. Mr. Brown, an engineer—I do not know that he is here—he gave me a regular notice to quit—I agreed to take 8 l. a year from the prisoner for three years from Michaelmas next—there were hardly any repairs to be made—I saw a labouring man with a brush in his hand—there were no wells to be emptied—the lead had been on the roof twenty or thirty years—it was not in a very had state and full of hole—I had no complaint of it—the engineer had used the place as a workshop—it had been an engineer's and was to be a cow-keeper's—it wanted no alteration—I had been over the place by myself two or three days before the prisoner entered, and saw the gutters and roof in a very satisfactory state; the prisoner and his wife went over the place with me—they did not go on to the roof.
LEONARD MATTHEWS. I was employed about the 10th of July by the prisoner on the premises in John-street, whitewashing—he afterwards employed me and Grander, a carpenter, to get the lead off the guttering and roof—I lent a hand to do so. I took the lead with a truck to Wright, a marinostore dealer, at 73, Redcross-street—we took four truck full of lead, and some in a sack—I only went once by myself with the truck—I received 11 s. 8 d., which I gave to Mr. Hatton on the premises—the other times the lead went I was outside—I went twice with Hatton, and twice with Grander—I took altogether about 9 cwt.
Cross-examined. Q. How long were you there? A. I went on a Tuesday, and left on the Saturday week following.
WILLIAM STEVENS. I removed from the premises in John-street some paving-stones which I purchased, I should think about four tons—it is a month ago—I paid 14 s. 6 d. for them to Grander, who was represented as foreman to Mr. Hatton.
ISAAC WOOD. I am foreman to Mr. Triggen, of Jewin-street—the prisoner employed me—I went and looked at the gutters on the 12th of July—I found they were stripped—I was to lay them with zinc—at the time I was looking I measured them, and found there were 270 or 280 feet—I said, "There is some lead gone off two of the lights"—he said, "That must be
put on"—I said we would do it—he said, "When will you come?"—I said, "To-morrow morning"—I went and put 2 cwt. of zinc, which came to 9 l. 7 s. 6 d.—I did not get paid for it.
THOMAS RICHARDS ( policeman M 44.) I took the prisoner on the 24th of July—I told him he was charged with taking the lead and stones—he said he had taken the premises, and employed a man named Grander, and he did not know what was became of him—he would give him into custody if he could find him.
NOT GUILTY.
(There were two other indictments against the prisoner, on which Mr. Parnell offered no evidence.)
[trialAccount t18470816-2005]
2005. GEORGE DURANT was indicted for stealing 11 shirts, value 1 l. 18 s.; 4 pair of stockings, 8 s.; 6 pairs of trowsers, 14 s; 1 pair of boots, 12 s.; 1 pair of shoes, 3 s.; 1 flannel jacket, 2 s. I jacket, 8 s.; 1 towel, 6 d. and 2 flannel shirts, 1 s. 6 d.; the goods of Reuben Whittle.
REUBEN WHITTLE. On Thursday, 5th of Aug., I had my stocking and trowsers, and the other property safe in my bag at the Three Goats' Heads, in the Wandsworth-road—I left them there in the landlady's charged—I asked if she would know me again—she said, "Yes."
Prisoner. You gave them to me on Thursday morning. Witness. I never gave them to him—these things produced are mine.
THOMAS FOSTER. I was at the public-house—I recollect these thing being left—the prisoner and Whittle were sitting outside drinking—the prisoner came the next morning for the bag—I thought it right to give it him—he was looking about in the washhouse and kitchen—I asked what he wanted—he said, "The soldier's bag"—I gave it him.
COURT to REUBEN WHITTLE. Q. Are you a soldier? A. I was discharged from the Royal Marines—I was not drinking with the prisoner—I wanted to shift myself—a man took me into a stable and told me I could shift there—I went and shifted, came out, took the bag into the public-house, and gave it to the landlady, and some papers too—I paid two pots of beer to the man who let me go into the stable—I did not tell the prisoner to go and get the things.
JOHN SAVILLE. I live at Bloomsbury. About one or two o'clock on Friday, the 6th of Aug., the prisoner came with the bag, and called for a cup of coffee—I saw him diving in the bag—he went out without drinking his coffee, which excited my suspicion—he said, "I will be here presently for the bag"—I turned it over, saw a government mark on it, and took it to the station—they told me to detain the boy when he came—he came, and I detained him—he said the soldier gave it him to mind, and he was going to take it to him.
prisoner's Defence. I met the prosecutor; he took me to a public-house; he gave me a pennyworth of gin; I went to several places with him, and then to the Three Goats' Heads; he gave me these things to mind.
GUILTY. Aged 15.— Confined Three Months.

2007. MARIA BROWN was indicted for feloniously receiving 3lbs. of gun-metal, value 2 s., the good of John Bennington Blyth.
JOHN BENNINGTON BLYTH. I am a brass-founder, and live in Guildford-street, Southwark. On the 13th of July Courtney came to my premises, and showed me some metal—it was mine, and had been stolen—we never sell it.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Can you swear to it? A. Yes—it was made on the 8th of July, and on the following Tuesday it was brought to me—my foreman knew of it on the Sunday—he has been bail for the prisoner—I have one of my sons in my employ—I have discharged one on account of this.
THOMAS BROWN. I made this metal on the Thursday before.
JAMES EVENS. On the 8th of July, about four o'clock in the afternoon, the prisoner came, and brought 3lbs. of metal to my shop—I bought it at 5 d. a pound—I bought some metal of her once before.
Cross-examined. Q. How long before? A. About a week or five days—I am sure she is the person who sold it me—I do not recollect buying any other metal that day, I bought bones and rags—I should think I made twenty or thirty purchases that day—I sold the metal to Courtney—I saw it afterwards at Mr. Blyth's.
JOHN COURTNEY. I bought the metal of Mr. Evens—I told the foreman of it on the Sunday afternoon.
JAMES BURTON ( policeman M 272.) I took the prisoner for receiving the metal—she denied all knowledge of it, and said she had never sold any metal at all.
NOT GUILTY.

[trialAccount t18470816-2011]
2011. HANNAH JENKINS was indicted for feloniously cutting and wounding George Trussell, with intent to do him some grievous bodily harm, (see page 578 and 589.)
MR. RAYLAND conducted the prosecution.
WILLIAM COCKING ( policeman L 108.) The prisoner was indicted, but not tried at the July Sessions—I took her into custody, on the 21st of July, on this charge, and I told her she was charged on an indictment, and a true bill had been returned at this Court for cutting and wounding George Trussell, at a house of ill fame in waterloo-road—she stated that she was in bed at the time, that she was tipsy, and heard Mary Ann Williams scream, "Murder!" and she ran down stair, and saw the prosecutor in the act of striking at Mary Ann with a poker; that she wrenched it from him, and took it away, but she never struck him with it.
Cross-examined by MR. PATNE. Q. Did she not say he was very violent, and trying to break a looking-glass? A. Yes, at the Magistrate's office, but not to me—this case was in the paper the first day of the Sessions—the prosecutor did not come, I was obliged to fetch him, I served him with a suspœns on Tuesday night.
GEORGE TRUSSELL. I am a labouring man, and live in Mint-street in the Borough. On Wednesday evening, the 9th of June, I was in St. Andrew's-terrace, near the Waterloo-road—I went to the house No.5, St. Andrew's-terrace, with Mary Ann Williams—she took a half-crown out of my pocket, and went up stair—I followed her—she came down again—I had a quarrel with her about it—the prisoner came down with her, and struck me over the head with poker—Williams tried to turn me out—I had not done anything to the prisoner before she struck me—the blow stunned me—I bled—when I recovered myself I was outside the house, on the povement—the doctor and two policemen were with me—I was taken to the hospital—it was about a fortnight before I was able to do any work.
Cross-examined. Q. You have made your appearance here twice before? A. Yes, I indicted Williams for stealing a half-crown; she was acquitted—I did not charged her with wounding me on the head—she struck me over the knees—she was found not guilty on both occasion—I swore she took the half-crown out of my pocket, and I saw it in her hand and tried to get it back and she would not give it me—I had been drinking a little before I went to the house—it was between four and five o'clock in the afternoon—I had spent about a shilling in beer and spirit before I went—I then sent for sixpenny-worth of gin when I was in the house—I had then a half-crown, two shilling, and one sixpence—I did not break open the parlour-door—I did not my I would break all the crockery—they said the next day that I broken the image on the mental-piece, and they were broken—I was in danger of my life—when the prisoner came down I was not flourishing a poker in front of the pier-glass—I did not strike Williams with that poker—the prisoner did not wrest the poker out of my hand—I did not say one ward about the halfcrown before they said I broke the image—this was in broad day-light, and in a street which the policeman pass up and down—I have not been to the prisoner's mother, and told her if she would give me 10 s. I would not appear, and if she did not I would try to transport her daughter—I did not offer to take 10 s.—her mother did not say she was too poor to give it me—the parties who kept the house sent for me—I cannot say whether I saw the prisoner mother—I might see several people—I did not offer to take 10 s. from
any woman—I swear I did not say that if the 10 s. was not paid, I would do all I could to transport this young woman.
JOHN THOMAS ( policeman L 58.) On the afternoon of the 9th of June I was called to St. Andrew's-terrace, Waterloo-road, and saw Trussell outside No. 5, in a chain, and bleeding very much from a wound on his head—he was insensible—a surgeon was sent for, and after some time he came to himself—I brought some women from the house No.5—the prisoner was not one of them, she made her escape from the back of the house before I was let in—she was not produced before the prosecutor that day at all.
Cross-examined. Q. When you got in, did you find Williams and three or four other females? A. Yes—I found 2 s. on Williams—Trussell smelt of liquor—I saw some broken images in the parlour of the house—it was after the people said that Trussell broke all their images, that he said Williams took the half-crown.
COURT. Q. Was the original charge for the blow he had received? A. Yes, and after that, he made the charge of the half-crown.
MR. PAYNE to WILLIAM COCKLING. Q. Where did you take the prisoner? A. She was taken at her mother's house, in Crown-street, Soho—I had been there before—I was specially employed by the superintendent of the L division—I had watched the house night and day for three weeks—I never saw her go in there myself, but sergeant Brooks had such a description of her from me, that he took her going into her mother's house—he brought her to me at my house directly from her mother's house—I was not present when she was apprehended—the man who took her is not here.
MR. RYLAND. Q. Did you see this deposition signed by Mr. Norton, the Magistrate? A. Yes—this is what the prisoner said—( read —"The prisoner on her examination says, 'What I said was, that I wrenched the tongs from his hand and left the room.'")
GUILTY of an Assault. Aged 19.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18470816-2012]
2012. SAMUEL SEWELL and JOHN BLOOMFIELD were indicted for stealing 64lbs. weight of bones, value 2 s. 4 d.; and 1 bag, 4 d.; the property of Joseph Maynard, the master of Bloomfield.
JOSEPH MAYNARD. I am a marine-store dealer, and live at 122, Cornwall-road, Lambeth—I occasionally employ Bloomfield—I came down on the morning of the 10th of July, in consequence of what my sister told me I went with Bywater into Sewell's room, and saw on the floor a bag with the bones in question—they were mine, they ought to be kept in my back warehouse—a passage leads there—there is no thoroughfare from the yard—I kept the key hanging up in my shop—it was found in Bloomfield's pocket—I told him what I had found in Sewell's room, and he took the key out of his pocket and delivered it to my sister-in-law—it was his usual custom to call me at seven o'clock, and I slept rather later through his not calling me—my sister lives next door—she came and called me, and told me something, and I went up to Sewell's room.
Bloomfield. He told me to shut up the warehouse the night before, and by doing that I, through mistake, put the key into my pocket. Wilness He dd shut up the warehouse the night before.
SARAH WILLIAMSON. I am single and live next door to Mr. Maynard—on the morning of the 10th of July I saw the prisoners in passage leading from the warehouse—Sewell had a bag in his hand, and Bloomfield had a key in his pocket—I saw Sewell going up stairs with the bag—I afterwards saw
it found in his room—I asked Bloomfield what he had done with the key, he made me no answer—he did not deliver it to me.
JOHN BYWATER ( policeman L 116.) I went with Mr. Maynard to Sewell's room, and heard him claim the bag of bones—I asked Sewell if it was his—he said "No"—I said, "Where did you get them?"—he said, "From down stairs, in Mr. Maynard's warehouse."
SEWELL'S DEFENCE. I never said any such thing; I was formerly employed as a porter in the Cut to clear gentlemen's house; there are often odds and ends left, such as rags and bones and bottles, that I have the privilege of having; I have offered such things for sale to Mr. Maynard; he cannot say otherwise.
JOSEPH MAYNARD re-examined. I never heard of his clearing out houses—I have bought a small quantity of things of him to the amount of one penny or three halfpence—I have missed things from time to time.
The prisoner Sewell called
DAVID NUNN. I know this bag to be the prisoner Sewell's—he brought it to put some little odds and ends in, to take to my new residence, when I was moving about seven month's ago
COURT. Q. How do you know that bag from one like it? A. It is very remarkable, it has the number 65 on it, I noticed that, as he put it on his shoulder—I believe that is the number—I have the piece which the bag was cut from, it is a piece of drugget—he had been in the habit of making hearthrugs—he had cut this piece out, and what he had left, he made a bag of.
COURT to JOSEPH MAYNARD. Q. How do you know the bag? A. I have missed a bag similar to this—I have lost it for two months.
JURY. Q. Was there any number on your bag? A. No.
JURY to DAVID NUNN. Q. Did you see the number on the bag? A. Yes, when Sewell moved the things from my place six or seven months ago, I thought it was a very remarkable number to be on a bag, number 65. ( The bag was opened, and the number on it was 59.)
Witness. I am positive it is the same bag, though I have been mistaken in the number.
JURY to JOSEPH MAYNARD. Q. Had your bag any number on it? A. I did not notice that it had—I cannot swear to the bones, the prisoners were seen coming through the passage—the warehouse runs down the passage—Sewell could take bones from the street to his own room—it is impossible for me to miss the quantity that he had, out of twelve or fourteen cwt. of bones.
COURT. Q. Where did you get the key? A. I cannot say in the hurry who gave it me.
Sewell. It is done through spite; a dog that the prosecutor had bit a large piece out of my boy's leg; I asked him for money to pay the doctor; he said at first that he would give me any recompense; I now and then got a sixpence from him, and that was all; if I could bring witnesses I could show that I got some bones from Mr. Lawrence, and some from Mr. Nunn, that very morning.
COURT to DAVID NUNN. Q. Had he any bones that morning of you, before nine o'clock? A. I cannot swear that I gave him them that morning—I had always given him bones on a Monday, and every week I have given him some—some were bones of beef—merely the bones from the domestic consumption, laid by for him—we had not a great deal of beef lately—perhaps we had it twice a-week—we accumulate a few bones—I do not know that I gave him any that morning; I might have done so the week before—I have not given him such a quantity as these.
JURY. Q. Is your family large? A. I have four—I put the bones by myself.
Bloomfield. I have several times found money behind the counter—I gave Mr. Maynard's father at different times about 4 s.—it is out of spite that he gave me in charge.
(Bloomfield received a good character.)
SEWELL GUILTY. Aged 34.— Confined One Month.
BLOOMFIELD GUILTY. Aged 18.— Confined Seven days.

2013. EVEN LLOYD was indicted for stealing 1 handkerchief, value 2 d.; 1 purse, 2 d.; 1 shilling; 1 sixpence; 1 token, value 1 halfpenny; and I half farthing; the goods of Ellen Cottering, from her person; 2nd COUNT, from a person whose name is unknown.
ALFRED SPICE ( policeman V 41.) On Sunday evening, the 1st of August, I saw the prisoner on the steam-boat pier, near the British Flag, at Battersea—a number of persons were waiting on the pier during part of the time—I watched the prisoner—a female came down on the pier—I saw him put his left hand into her pocket—I got by the side of him, and caught him hand as he pulled it out, with a handkerchief and purse in it—he dropped it, made a stroke at it with his foot, and tried to kick it overboard—Gibbs picked it up—the female came off the boat, claimed the purse and handkerchief, and said, "You want me for a future day; I shall not come"—there was a shilling, a sixpence, and two copper coins in the purse.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. There were sixty or seventy persons there? A. Yes; it was between six and seven o'clock—I was on the dummy close to the prisoner, in plain clothes—I was on the outside of the crowd—the female gave her name to my brother constable—I heard her say, "Cottering" I think it was, but I will not swear what it was.
WILLIAM GIBBS ( policeman V 77.) I saw the prisoner take his hand from the female's pocket—Spice took hold of his left hand, he dropped the handkerchief and purse, which I took up, and produce—the young woman gave the name of "Ellen Cottering"—she spelt it to me—there was a shilling, a sixpence, a half farthing, and a copper coin in the purse.

GUILTY. † Aged 17— Confined Nine Months.

[trialAccount t18470816-2016]
2016. JOSEPH GLISSEN was indicted for stealing 1 sovereign; 1 half-crown; and 1 order for the payment of 9 l. 15 s.; the moneys of Joseph Barnes, from the person of Sarah Barnes.
MR. ROBINSON conducted the Prosecution.
SARAH BARNES. I am the wife of Joseph Barnes, and live at Walworth. On Thursday evening, the 13th of July, I was in the Walworth-road, at nearly eleven o'clock—I passed up to Walworth-terrace—the place was quite clear, there was no one before me—all at once two men came from behind and prevented my walking on—one kept before me and kept looking in my face, the other was by my side—I had my hand on my pocket, and presently my shawl got off—one of the men looking very impudently in my face caused me to look towards the terrace—if I went one way to avoid them, they went the same way—the prisoner is not the one who looked in my face, he kept close at my side, and the other just before me—in a moment I was left quite by myself—I put my hand into my pocket, and my purse was gone—I had but a few moments before been speaking to Mr. Tarry, and given him a little order—I had my purse then—I am sure the prisoner is one of the men that was there—he had a dark coat on, either black or blue—I lost this check, a sovereign, a half-crown, and my purse—I went the next morning to the Bank to stop the check, and saw the prisoner there—I was there from a quarter of an hour to twenty minutes, waiting in an inner room—as soon as the check was presented, a gentleman brought it to me—I said it was mine—that was exactly nine o'clock, the moment the Bank opened.
Cross-examined by MR. PARRY. Q. When do you say you were robbed? A.> On the 13th of July, between a quarter to eleven and eleven o'clock in the evening—I am quite certain of the time—it was near Manor-place, in the Walworth-road, near the Zoological-gardens—I only saw two persons—I am not aware that there were any others—I had been speaking to Mr. Tarry about two minutes before—this was done in a moment—I believe I should be able to identify the other man—I am not more positive now than I was—I knew the prisoner again the moment I saw him—I went into the office, I did not know that the prisoner was the man that presented the check; but as soon as I saw his back and side face I said he was one of the men that robbed me—the other man was dressed in dark clothes, whether black or blue I cannot tell—he had a coat on—it appeared to me like a frock coat—I have been twice before the Magistrate—the bill has been preferred here, and no depositions taken—the prisoner, when at the Magistrate's office, called witnesses—he called John Dawson and Ambrose Dickinson—it was after hearing those witnesses that the Magistrate refused to commit, and we came before the Grand Jury—I deposed there as I have done here to-day.
MR. ROBINSON. Q. When you were in the banking-house the prisoner was there with two or three other persons? A. There were more—I selected the prisoner from the rest of them—he was not at all pointed out.
THOMAS TIBBS. I am a clerk at the Borough Bank. On the 14th of July, at a little after nine o'clock, this check was presented by the prisoner—I was present when he was identified by Mrs. Barnes.
Witnesses for the Defence.
JOHN DAWSON. I keep a beer-shop, at No. 37, Tower-street. Waterloo-road. I know the prisoner—I believe he keeps a coffee-house in Waterloo-road—on the evening of the 13th of July he came to my house from twenty minutes to a quarter to ten o'clock—I would not be positive to five minutes—he had a bucket with him, and asked me if I would give him a bucket of water—I told him to go behind and fetch it—he used to come and fetch water from my house occasionally, when he was short of water—I have kept this beer-shop ever since the 5th if April—I told him he might fetch the water—he
went in to the tap-room, put the bucket down, and said, "I think I will have a pint of porter"—I served him—he stood at the bar and drank it—there were many persons came in for their supper-beer—he remained in my shop up to twelve o'clock, till shutting-up time—I was there all that time—it was impossible for me to leave—I am sure the prisoner was there during the whole of that time, up to the closing—a next door neighbour, Mr. Dickindon, came in—the prisoner asked him if he would have a drink of his porter, which he did, and then Mr. Dickinson called for another, which, to the best or my recollection, was pint of stout, and then he said he would have a pipe of tobacco, and asked me to give him a pipe, which I did—he had his own tobacco—he was there drinking, smoking, and talking, all that time—when he left, he left with the bucket; he went behind, fetched the water, and went home—I do not know that I know Manor-place, Walworth-road—I am not much acquainted with London—the prisoner came from Bath, the same town as I did—I have known him from a child—I never knew anything against his character—I was examined before the Magistrate, and Mr. Dickinson also—they came to my house the next morning, and said the prisoner was apprehended for a robbery that took place at eleven o'clock.
MR. ROBINSON. Q. Have you always given the same account of this? A. Yes—I made the same statement before the Magistrate that I have today, as far as I can recollect—Walworth-road is on the other side of the Elephant and Castle; I do not know how far it is from where I live—I should go up the Waterloo-road and down the London-road to go to the Elephant and Castle—it would take me more than five minutes to walk, I should say ten—the prisoner lives about fifty yards from me—he came to his house since I have been in mine—I do not know what sort of a house his coffee-house is—I have been in there merely to say, "Good morning." by knowing him—I never sat down in his house—it was about twenty minutes to ten o'clock when the prisoner came in that evening—he left his bucket in the tap-room, and then came to talk to me at the bar—he did not hurry himself in drinking the porter—I did not keep an account how long he was there—many persons came into my house; I served them—my wife was ill in bed—there was nobody to serve but myself—I had to take things into the tap-room to several persons—there was no one to serve the people at the bar and in the tap-room but me—I could not say how many persons came into my house from a quarter before ten o'clock till the close; I never kept an account, I should say thirty, or about that—I do not remember any of them coming to drink at the bar but Dickinson—I do not know what time he came in—the prisoner had only got his pint of porter before Dickinson entered—he remained till shutting-up time, twelve o'clock—that is the usual time for closing—he and the prisoner talked together—the prisoner asked for an empty pipe—he brought his own tobacco—I said I would take a little tobacco—there is a form there—whether the prisoner sat down or not I cannot tell—Dickinson did not sit down that I am aware of, I cannot be positive—they continued talking the whole time—when you go in at my door, the bar is straight in front, so that when I am serving in the bar I am looking into the street—I did not watch to see whether the prisoner and Dickinson stood in the same position the whole time—they did not go out of the bar—they were just in the position persons would be who came to ask for a pint of beer—I never measured my bar—I suppose it is about fifteen feet wide, from end to end—the part where I serve customers is about four feet—I was standing at the engine, smoking and drawing beer—I cannot recollect what we were talking about—my attention was called to this the next morning, and that makes me
recollect what took place—I cannot tell any of the conversation, only we had a little laugh over a little tobacco—he asked me for a pipe—I gave him a pipe and brought one for myself—I said I would take a little tobacco—Dickinson said, "That is diamond cut diamond; you find tobacco, and Dawson find pipes"—that is all I recollect—the prisoner did not tell me what he wanted the water for—I do not know what time his house closes; I never heard that from him—I do not drive such a very good trade in the evening—I never have any one from the theatres—my house lies back—I have known Dickinson ever since I have been there—I kept a public-house at Bristol two years—I left, because I did not like it—I went to Bath, and came up to London last March—I was born and bred at Bath—I was a butcher there many years—that business would have answered very well to those who liked it, but I did not—there was no other reason why I changed from the butchering business to a publican—I was only a journeyman butcher—I kept the public-house on my own account.
MR. PARRY. Q. Did you run away from Bath? A. No—the public-house I kept at Bristol was like the house I now keep—I had no spirit license—on this night I was standing attending to my business, Dickinson and the prisoner were talking—I occasionally spoke to them—I was not more busy that night than on other nights—it was Tuesday night.
COURT. Q. If I understand you right, both the prisoner and Dickinson were there nearly two hours? A. Yes, the prisoner came first, and a short time afterwards Dickinson arrived—I cannot tell whether they sat down, or what they talked about—they stood at the further end of the bar, away from the door—to the best of my recollection they were standing all the time—they were not exactly near me when they were smoking—they were about six feet off—they had only one pipe—whether they filled it more than once I cannot say—as near as I can recollect Dickinson went out first, but not above a minute before the prisoner—that was just before twelve o'clock—I closed near upon twelve.
AMBROSE DICKINSON. I am a cane-dealer, for chairs and other things—I live at 38, Tower-street, Waterloo-road—I have lived there fourteen years, and carried on that business next to Mr. Dawson's beer-shop—I do not know how long he has been there, I suppose about three months—on the evening of the 13th of July, the prisoner and I were in his bar—the prisoner was there when I went in, which as near as I can recollect was about ten o'clock, I had just had my supper and shut my shop—the prisoner was standing at the bar—he had a pint of porter in his hand—he offered me the mug—I drank with him—we laughed and joked the best part of an hour over his porter—when that was out, I called for a pint of stout—we drank that together till it was just upon twelve o'clock—he and I were laughing about some tobacco—I did not smoke, the prisoner and the landlord did—the prisoner had got his tobacco, and the landlord asked him for a bit, and said it was better than his—I said that was diamond cut diamond, between the two—there was no one else conversing with us—the prisoner and I were standing at the bar—my wife came in two or three times—she was attending on the landlord's wife, who was dying—she is now dead—Dawson served his customers at the time he was joking with us—there were no other persons with us, there were some in the tap-room, but I did not notice them—I left first—I did not see the prisoner come out—I did not see any bucket—I was examined before the Magistrate—my attention was called to this on the following day; the prisoner's wife came in—he was then in custody on this charge—I went directly to the office, and gave my evidence.
MR. ROBINSON. Q. What made her come to you? A. To ask if I was in his company the night before—I have only been two or three times in his company—that was in the beer-shop—I have met him there three or four times—I never was in his company so long before that—I was not in his company the evening before that—it might have been a week or two before—I cannot remember the conversation—we were joking about one thing or another—about his clothes—Dawson took a part in it—I do not remember that he left the bar, only serving his customers—several neighbours came in for their beer—the prisoner never left me—he did not stand all the time, there was a form brought and we sat down—I did not notice who brought the form in—I do not know whether I asked for the form—I know all the bear we had was one pint of porter, and one of stout—I am no drinker—I know it was twelve o'clock when I went away, because that is the time he generally puts the shutters up, and I go to bed—that is the time we generally go out—I am in the habit of going in there to have a pint of ale—twelve o'clock at night is rather a busy time in the Waterloo-road, a great many people are going to get refreshment.
MR. PARRY. Q. Have you any doubt that the prisoner was in your company? A. No; he is as innocent as I am—I am ill, and came out of bed now to tell the truth—my wife is at home, doing her business for her children—I have no doubt about her having been three or four times in the beer-shop.
MATILDA BOWLER. I am a widow, and reside at No.30, Waterloo-road, next door to the prisoner—I am a green-grocer and fishmonger. On the night of the 13th July, I saw the prisoner pass my door in his shirt-sleeves, about eleven or a quarter to eleven o'clock—his wife came out about half-past eleven—she asked if I had seen either Joe or Glissen, as he was gone for a bucket of water—at ten minutes to twelve he came past with a pail of water, and I said, "You will get your hair combed; your wife has been here."
MR. ROBINSON Q. At a quarter to eleven o'clock he went out for a pail of water, and at ten minutes to twelve o'clock he returned and you spoke to him? A. Yes.
MR. ROBINSON called
JOHN BAKER ( policeman M 72.) I knew nothing of the prisoner till I took him into custody on this charge; but his house is frequented by the worst of characters; being open all night—it is a coffee and lodging-house—he lets beds as well.
MR. PARRY. Q. Have you reported this house, that you swear is the resort of bad characters. A. No; I have had a knowledge of the house nine months—the prisoner has not kept it all that time—I believe he kept it about three weeks previous to his being taken into custody—the attorney was employed in this prosecution at the commencement of this Session—I cannot mention the name of any one thief, whom I have seen coming out of that coffee-house—I have never been in the house during the time the prisoner has been there.
William Rogers, publican of Drury-lane; Ambrose Pocock, butcher of Bath; and Stephen Baily, salesman of Newgate-market; gave the prisoner a good character.
GUILTY. — Recommended mercy by the Jury. — Transported for Seven Years
[trialAccount t18470816-2017]
2017. JOSEPH GROVE was indicted for stealing 91 lbs. weight of sugar, value 1 l. 17 s.; the goods of John Coles, his master.
MR. PARNELL conducted the Prosecution.
THOMAS PAUL. I am a custom-house locker, doing duty in the sugar department, at the London Docks. On the 27th of July, four hogsheads of sugar were weighed—one of them was marked "No.7., I.P. M. S. G."—the weight of it was 16 cwt. 2 qrs. 21lbs.—I only took the weight on the part of the Crown.
JOHN BARBER. I am a grocer, and live at Lower Marsh-gate, Lambeth. On the 27th of July, I ordered some hogsheads of sugar to be fetched from the docks—one of them was marked "No.7., I.P.M.S.G."—Mr. Coles was ordered to bring them—my porter Swaincott assisted him—they were delivered at ordered my house, and pitched up on end—I was engaged at the time they were delivered—I afterwards found the weight of that hogshead was 15 cwt. 3 qrs, 14 lbs.—that was 3 qrs. and 7 lbs. short, making 91 lbs. deficient—the others were the right weight—I saw the prisoner unload the hogsheads—they were covered and perfectly secured—there was a piece of iron nailed over the drawing hole—I examined them, and I am confident the one on this hogshead had been removed—my father is in partnership with me—the prisoner did not put any nose-bag on the horses, when he was unloading—it is mostly done.
PHILIP SWAINCOTT. On the 27th of July I helped the prisoner to unload the wagon—I lifted the tarpaulin, to get a bag to put on my shoulder—the prisoner said, "There is nothing there but my old rug"—his hand was on the tarpaulin as soon as I touched it—I could not see what was under it, but I put my foot, and there was a lump of something like sugar in the wagon—I got the hogsheads in—I did not open either of them—he offered to help me to open them—I said, "I shall not open them now, I am busy"—I saw this one weighed afterwards.
Cross-examined by Mr. BALLANTINE. Q. You got into the wagon as soon as it came up? A. I went in to assist with the hogsheads—it was perhaps ten minutes before I went to speak to my master—he was in the shop all the time.
MR. PARNELL. Q. Where was this sugar drawn from? A. From the drawing hole.
JOHN COLES. I am a carman. The prisoner was in my employ—on the 27th of July I employed him to do Mr. Barber's work—I afterwards missed a nose-bag—I was in bed when they came and told about this—my wife called me up, and I said to the prisoner, " Joe, here is another serious case"—I got an officer, and gave him in charge.
Cross-examined. Q. How long had he been with you? A. Nine years last month.
MR. BALLANTINE to THOMAS PAUL. Q. Did you weigh these hogsheads yourself? A. I saw them weighed—I sit in the box, and take the weight—somebody calls the weight to me, but I see the weights, and see that they call correctly—I can see the scale out of the window of the box—I can see the weight to a pound.
EDWARDS ORAMS. I am delivery-foreman at the London Docks. The figures of this delivery note are my writing—I delivered these hogsheads to the prisoner, and they were all right weight.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you weigh them? A. I saw them weighed—I
recollect seeing these weighed, and seeing that they were correct—they were delivered to the prisoner between three and four o'clock—I gave directions to the men to put them into the wagon, and saw the prisoner take them in after they were weighed.
GUILTY. Aged 39.— Confined Nine Months.

2019. JAMES JACKSON TICKELL was indicted for stealing 79 sovereigns, the moneys of John Taylor, from his person.
(The prosecutor did not appear.)
NOT GUILTY.
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, SEPTEMBER 20 TH, 1847.
